 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

1.1 Background

 About 50 million gallons of high-level mixed waste is currently stored in underground
tanks at the United States Department of Energy’s (DOE’s) Hanford site in the State of
Washington. The Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP) will provide
DOE’s Office of River Protection (ORP) with a means of treating this waste by vitrification for
subsequent disposal. The tank waste will be separated into low- and high-activity waste
fractions, which will then be vitrified respectively into Immobilized Low Activity Waste
(ILAW) and Immobilized High Level Waste (IHLW) products. The ILAW product will be
disposed in an engineered facility on the Hanford site while the IHLW product is designed for
acceptance into a national deep geological disposal facility for high-level nuclear waste. The
ILAW and IHLW products must meet a variety of requirements with respect to protection of the
environment before they can be accepted for disposal.

 The Office of River Protection is examining options to optimize the Low Activity Waste
(LAW) Facility and LAW glass waste form. One option under evaluation is to enhance the waste
processing rate of the vitrification plant currently under construction. It is likely that the capacity
of the LAW vitrification plant can be increased incrementally by implementation of a variety of
low-risk, high-probability changes, either separately or in combination. The Vitreous State
Laboratory (VSL) at The Catholic University of America (CUA) and EnergySolutions, Inc. have
evaluated several potential incremental improvements for ORP in support of its evaluation of
WTP LAW facility optimization [1]. Some of these incremental improvements have been tested
at VSL, including increasing the waste loading, increasing the processing temperature, and
increasing the fraction of the sulfur in the feed that is partitioned to the off-gas (assuming that the
present WTP recycle loop can be broken) [2-4]. These approaches successfully demonstrated
increases in glass production rates and significant increases in sulfate incorporation at the
nominal melter operating temperature of 1150C and at slightly higher than nominal glass
processing temperatures. Subsequent tests demonstrated further enhancement of glass
formulations for all of the LAW waste envelopes, thereby reducing the amount of glass to be
produced by the WTP for the same amount of waste processed [5, 6]. The next phase of testing
determined the applicability of these improvements over the expected range of sodium and sulfur
concentrations for Hanford LAW [7]. This approach was subsequently applied to an even wider
range of LAW wastes types, including those with high potassium concentration [8, 9]. These
high waste loading formulations employed new glass additives, such as SnO2 and V2O5, in place
primarily of Fe2O3 and TiO2 [7-9].

 The 2012 Tank Utilization Assessment (TUA) [10] employed the current baseline WTP
LAW glass models [11] to evaluate scenarios in which the WTP and second LAW facilities are
coupled but with finite second LAW capacity. These scenarios resulted in a significant increase

 10

in the amount of LAW glass produced as well as extensive idling of the High Level Waste
(HLW) facility. The primary cause appears to be the buildup of halides, and to lesser extent
sulfates, in the recycle stream, which leads to reduced waste loading in the LAW glass. The
reduced waste loading increases the glass volume, which reduces the waste processing rate
through the second LAW facility, which in turn holds up operation of the WTP HLW facility.
The TUA calculations assume the same WTP baseline LAW glass models for both the WTP
facility and the second LAW facility. These models determine the waste loading for each batch
of feed material, the composition of which is determined by that of the LAW feed and the
recycle stream. The LAW glass models essentially consist of two parts: the LAW Correlation
[12], and a set of specific constraints for halides, chromate, and phosphate [13], which are based
on extensive crucible and melter testing at various scales (up to and including one-third scale
LAW Pilot Melter) performed by VSL and EnergySolutions for Bechtel National, Inc. (BNI).
Previous assessments showed that use of these models in the WTP allowed the WTP contract
LAW loadings to be met. However, those assessments focused on the performance of the WTP
and included secondary LAW treatment only as a simple “black-box” with infinite capacity for
“excess” LAW. In view of the LAW glass development work performed for ORP [2-9], it is
likely that revised glass models would provide higher waste loadings than the baseline WTP
models, which could potentially mitigate the highly undesirable consequences found in the 2012
TUA projections. Such a revision would build upon relevant data with the appropriate WTP QA
levels that have been collected since the development of the baseline models, as well as the
targeted collection of new data to both fill data gaps and to validate the new models. While there
are significant existing data sets that could be used to increase the sulfate and sodium loadings
achieved in the revised models, additional testing on scaled melters needs to be performed to
address the halide, chromate, and phosphate limits in view of the observed tendency to form
secondary salt phases while processing WTP baseline glass compositions at elevated halide
concentrations [14-16]. In addition, as with the original development of the baseline models, the
revised models would need to be validated by running melter tests on a series of melter feed
compositions that are calculated with the revised models to demonstrate processability, product
quality, and prevention of salt phase formation.

 The present work represents the second of two phases to address the formation of
secondary sulfate phases while processing high waste loading LAW formulations with elevated
halide, chromium, and phosphate feed concentrations over a wide range of LAW waste types,
including those with high potassium concentrations. The Test Plan for the present work [17]
outlines a series of tests designed to assess the formation of secondary phases over a range of
sulfur and halide concentrations for two LAW streams and associated glass formulations as well
as crucible testing to identify additives that have the potential to suppress the formation of
secondary phases for wastes with high chlorine contents. The combined results from both phases
of testing and from previous tests with these LAW waste streams will be used as inputs to future
work to develop concentration limits for these components in the LAW glass property-
composition models and to support the modification and updating of the current WTP LAW
glass formulation algorithm [18, 19].

 11

1.2 WTP Baseline, Current Waste Stream Projections, and Enhanced Glass
 Formulations

 Under a separate contract to support the WTP Project, VSL has developed and tested
glass formulations for the WTP to provide data to meet the WTP contract requirements and to
support system design activities [11, 20-24]. That work is based upon small-scale batch melts
(“crucible melts”) using waste simulants. Selected formulations have also been tested in
small-scale, continuously-fed, joule-heated melters (DM10 and DM100 systems) [25-36] and,
ultimately, in the LAW Pilot Melter [37-48]. Such melter tests provide information on key
process factors such as feed processing behavior, dynamic effects during processing, sulfate
incorporation, processing rates, off-gas amounts and compositions, foaming control, etc., that
cannot be reliably obtained from crucible melts. This sequential scale-up approach in the
vitrification testing program ensures that maximum benefit is obtained from the more costly
melter tests and that the most effective use is made of those resources.

 Under the WTP support effort, VSL and EnergySolutions have developed and identified
glass compositions for processing the Phase I LAW tank streams for the WTP. These
compositions have been tested for processing and product quality requirements at various scales
ranging from crucible melts of about 400 g up to the LAW Pilot Melter at processing rates in
excess of 6600 kg/day (2000 kg/m2/day). The testing included the nominal feed compositions
and those with 15% variations in the waste simulants added to the melter feeds. The melter
testing provided high confidence that the selected WTP compositions are unlikely to cause
accumulation of a separate sulfate phase in the melter even at high feed processing rates. Feed
processing characteristics and off-gas characteristics have been determined at various melter
scales and data have been collected to support engineering and permitting requirements.
Furthermore, statistically designed composition matrices were generated and crucible melts of
those glass compositions were prepared and characterized to qualify the glass composition region
around the baseline LAW glass compositions selected for WTP waste processing [11, 24]. As a
result of the considerable testing completed with the WTP baseline formulations, there is high
confidence that they can be used to process LAW at Hanford with little additional testing. The
recommended glass compositions for LAW processing were selected such that they can tolerate
process variations without adverse effects on processing or product quality. Based on these
well-tested formulations, VSL developed a LAW glass formulation correlation to support WTP
operations [12]. The WTP baseline formulations were developed to comply with the
requirements of the BNI contract with ORP [49]. The selected WTP compositions have also been
tested to ensure their compatibility with melter materials of construction. The glass formulation
development and melter testing work for the selected WTP compositions have reached a level of
maturity where the compositions can be used for waste processing at the WTP with relatively
high confidence.

 The WTP baseline formulations were also evaluated for the sensitivity to the presence of
varying amounts of halides in the LAW streams with respect to the formation of secondary salt
phases [14, 15]. Although glass formulations generated by the LAW glass formulation algorithm
(including process uncertainties in the worst-case direction) that span a wide range of sodium
and sulfate concentrations can be processed without forming separate sulfur phases on the melt

 12

surface, salt phase formation resulted when these formulations were processed with elevated
levels of chromium or halides within the ranges resulting from WTP flow-sheet projections. The
formation of sulfate phases is exacerbated by potassium and mitigated by phosphorus [14, 15].
The results provided part of the basis for development of additional constraints that can be added
to the glass formulation algorithm to address these effects.

 The deleterious consequences of molten salt formation are well-recognized and include
potential impacts on melter lifetime and operations as well as on product quality. Since the WTP
has no means of detecting the formation of molten salt phases, the mitigation strategy involves
testing to determine the conditions under which such phases form, development of appropriate
composition controls, and implementation of those composition controls during operations to
avoid salt phase formation. The LAW glass formulation correlation [12] provided the basis for
the development of such a compositional control scheme for the operation of the WTP LAW
vitrification system. Tests assessing the limitations of that correlation clearly showed that, based
on the projected ranges of feed compositions with elevated halide and chromium concentrations,
there is a significant residual risk of formation of molten salt phases during operations, with all
of the attendant deleterious consequences, if the correlation is implemented without
modification. There was therefore a clear need to incorporate additional constraints to address
the effects of chlorine, fluorine, chromium, phosphorus, and potassium, which was addressed for
the WTP baseline in subsequent testing [14, 15] and use of the resulting data to develop such
constraints [13].

1.3 Enhanced Glass Formulations

 VSL and EnergySolutions have previously developed and tested LAW glass formulations
for ORP [4-9] that are distinct from those developed for the WTP baseline. LAW testing for
ORP at VSL was aimed at optimizing the glass formulations and processing parameters in order
to minimize the volume of glass produced and to shorten the plant operating schedule. An
important objective was to determine the likely limits of LAW loading across the full range of
expected LAW compositions. Increases in waste loadings were achieved for six representative
Hanford LAW streams; a compilation of the target sodium, potassium, sulfur, and overall waste
loading increases over WTP baseline compositions is provided in Table 1.1. An overview of the
Na2O and SO3 loadings for the WTP and ORP glasses is shown in Figure 1.1. As is evident from
Figure 1.1 and Table 1.1, these glasses represent considerable increases in waste loadings and
sulfur contents over the WTP baseline and therefore considerable potential for reductions in cost
and schedule. Importantly, these enhancements should be directly applicable to the planned
supplemental LAW vitrification facility. Furthermore, since the approach adopted in the work for
ORP spans likely extremes in the expected LAW compositions, the results would remain
relevant even if various LAW tank wastes are blended, since blending generally has the effect of
affording yet further improvements in waste loadings.

 The melter testing described in this report and a previous report [16] was designed to
investigate the effects of halide, chromium, and phosphate concentrations on secondary salt
phase formation in the enhanced waste loading LAW glass formulations. The high waste loading

 13

glass formulations selected for this work are those developed for LAW from tanks AP-101,
AN-105, AN-102, AN-107, and AZ-101 since these cover the range of sulfate to sodium and
potassium to sodium ratios expected for Hanford LAW [7-9]. The same five wastes were
previously tested as part of the basis for the LAW correlation using the corresponding WTP
baseline glass formulations (LAWE3, LAWE4H, LAWE5H, LAWE7H, and LAWE10H,
respectively) in terms of the tendency for salt formation over a range of halide, chromium, and
phosphate concentrations [14, 15]. The present testing provides similar data for the enhanced
high waste loading formulations. This information, in combination with the extensive results
from the previous studies, will provide ORP with a basis for evaluation of the magnitude of
potential future enhancements of the WTP over and above the present well-developed baseline.

1.4 Test Objectives

 As described in the Test Plan for this work [17], the primary objective of the present
work was to collect data required to support revision of the present baseline LAW glass
algorithms [18, 19] that are used to project the performance of both the WTP and second LAW
facilities. Such a revision has the potential to substantially increase waste loadings and reduce
LAW glass volume. In work supported by ORP over the past several years, VSL and
EnergySolutions have collected extensive data on new LAW glass formulations that have
increased sulfate and sodium loadings. These data, together with those from the baseline glass
models, would form the basis for the proposed model revisions. The data collected in this work
will be used to support revision of the models to increase the limits for halides. The primary
objective of the present work was to further evaluate the tolerance to higher halide
concentrations of the high waste loading glass formulations that were developed in the earlier
work [7, 8] for two LAW compositions specified by ORP [50]. One of the wastes has the highest
waste organic content expected for Hanford LAW wastes; the other waste contains the highest
expected chlorine content in Hanford LAW wastes and was evaluated for a different enhanced
glass formulation than previously studied [16]. The DM10 was used for several previous tests on
LAW compositions [2-9, 15, 16] to determine the maximum feed sulfur concentrations that can
be processed without forming secondary sulfate phases on the surface of the melt pool. This
melter is the most efficient melter platform for screening glass compositions over a wide range
of concentrations and therefore was selected for the present tests.

 The two waste types selected for testing, the corresponding glass compositions [7, 8], and
their respective sodium contents are given below.

  ORPLA20 (based on LAW tank AN-105): 31.4 wt% waste loading, 24.0 wt% Na2O.

  ORPLB4 (based on LAW tank AN-107) 26.2 wt% waste loading, 24.0 wt% Na2O.

Specific test objectives were to [17]:

  Process glass formulations that are compliant with processing and product quality
 requirements for the above two waste streams at high waste loadings.

 14

  Conduct small-scale melter testing (DM10) to evaluate the chloride tolerance for the
 above glass formulations that have been shown to achieve higher sulfate and sodium
 loadings at nominal halides levels.

  Determine the maximum feed chloride concentrations that can be processed on the DM10
 melter without the formation of secondary sulfate phases for each of the two LAW glass
 formulations at three different sulfur concentrations.

  Assess the effect of glass formulation on the formation of secondary sulfate phases for a
 high chlorine Hanford LAW waste.

  Assess the effect of different additives on chlorine retention and secondary phase
 formation using crucible-scale melts.

  Use the data from DM10 testing to support the development of revised models.

  Assess the implications on waste loading of secondary phase formation resulting from
 halides.

 The present work was preceded by an initial phase of testing, described in a separate
report [16]. The combined results from both test phases will be used as inputs in future work to
develop concentration limits for these components in the LAW glass property-composition
models and to support the modification and updating of the current WTP LAW glass formulation
algorithm.

1.5 Crucible Testing Overview

 Crucible scale tests were conducted to screen possible additives to the LAW glass
formulations for their potential effects on decreasing the tendency for secondary phase formation
in the presence of higher halide concentrations. The AN-105 LAW waste and ORPLA20 glass
composition provided the basis for the compositional variations that were evaluated. Glass frits
based on the ORPLA20 glass composition were produced without MgO, Fe2O3, SnO2 and
reduced CaO, which together contribute 5 wt%; the frits also excluded a portion of the sodium in
order to accommodate the addition of variable amounts of sodium chloride while maintaining the
target sodium content of 24 wt% Na2O. Variations for testing were generated by blending the
equivalent of 5.0 wt% of the constituent to be tested (i.e., CoO, CuO, Fe2O3, Fe3O4, MnO2, NiO,
SnO2, V2O5 or P2O5 [17]), glass frit, and the amount of NaCl required to achieve the desired
chlorine content. The blends were melted in a closed crucible system; the product was then
inspected for secondary phase formation and analyzed to determine chloride and sulfur solubility
for each blend tested. All of the compositions were evaluated at relatively high chloride contents
in the screening tests. Based on the results obtained during the closed crucible solubility tests,
further test were conducted for those additives that enhanced salt accommodation. The overall

 15

best formulation was characterized for the key performance and processing properties. Details of
the crucible tests are given in Section 3.0.

1.6 DM10 Test Overview

 Seventeen different tests using the DM10 melter system were conducted to determine the
concentration of chloride that results in the formation of a secondary sulfate phase on the glass
pool surface for each of the two glass compositions at three different sulfur contents. The testing
was designed such that the impact of chlorine on sulfate phase formation could be determined in
the alkali-limited region. These tests were performed at 1150C and with a glass production rate
of 2000 - 2250 kg/m2/day. Each test segment was of 14-16 hours duration, which corresponds to
about three melter turnovers. For each waste composition and sulfur content, the chloride
concentration was progressively increased to the point at which a sulfate salt phase developed,
indicating the limit of sulfate and chloride incorporation for that particular formulation. Key
operating parameters including cold cap coverage, feed rate, and glass pool temperature were
held constant to investigate the effects of each of the feed compositions on processing
characteristics (including salt formation) and the product glass. The bubbling rate was adjusted to
provide the desired feed rate with a complete cold cap (90-100% of melt surface covered with
feed). Quantitative measurements of glass production rates, melter operating conditions
(temperatures, pressures, power, flows, etc.), and off-gas characteristics (NOX, SO2, CO, CO2,
and NH3) were made for each test.

 The simulated melter feeds were prepared for each of the two compositions in such a way
that the halide, sulfur, and chromium contents could be varied. Sugar was added to the feed to
achieve the equivalent of a nominal stoichiometric ratio of 0.5 (1 mole sucrose per 16 moles
NOx) to the remaining nitrate after accounting for the redox reactions with carbon supplied by
the waste organics (which are also assumed at a 0.5 stoichiometric ratio).

1.7 Quality Assurance

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program compliant with 10 CFR 830
Subpart A, NQA-1 (2004), and DOE Order 414.1C. This program was supplemented by a
Quality Assurance Project Plan (QAPP) for ORP work [51] that is conducted at VSL. Test and
procedure requirements by which the testing activities were planned and controlled are defined
in the Test Plan [17]. The program is supported by VSL standard operating procedures (SOPs)
that were used for this work [52]. The requirements of DOE/RW-0333P are not applicable to this
work.

 SECTION 2.0
 WASTE SIMULANTS, GLASS FORMULATIONS AND FEED ANALYSIS

2.1 Waste Simulants

 The Hanford tank waste simulants that were used were based on the composition data for
tanks AN-105 and AN-107 given in Tables 2.1 and 2.2. These are the same waste simulants
previously used for development of the respective high waste loading glass compositions and
determination of sulfur solubility while processing on the DM10 [7-9].

 2.1.1 Hanford Tank AN-105 Waste Simulant

 The AN-105 waste simulant is the same as that used in the earlier studies on AN-105 [6,
7], which was based on the composition data for tank AN-105 given in a WTP Test Specification
[53]; however, the sulfate concentration was increased from 0.6 to 2.7 wt% SO3 in order to reach
the sulfur loading requirements for the glass formulation [8, 9]. The base waste composition
incorporates Tank Farm Contractor Operation and Utilization Plan (TFCOUP) [54] data, actual
waste analysis data, and WTP flow sheet information. The sodium concentration in the simulant
includes a 2.5 % increase to account for sodium additions in pretreatment [20, 55]. The nominal
concentration, expressed in terms of the sodium molarity, was determined on the basis of melter
feed rheology tests on similar formulations [56, 57]. The results of those tests led to the selection
of 8.0 molar sodium as the nominal simulant concentration for the LAW AN-105 waste. This is
the same concentration that was used for previous WTP melter tests for LAW AN-105 waste [26,
34]. The LAW AN-105 simulant is a solution of predominantly sodium, aluminum, nitrate,
nitrite, and sulfate, as shown in Table 2.1.

 2.1.2 Hanford Tank AN-107 Waste Simulant

 The AN-107 waste simulant was based on the composition data for tank AN-107 given in
a WTP Test Specification [53]. The base waste composition incorporates TFCOUP [54] data,
actual waste analysis data, and WTP flow sheet information. The sodium concentration in the
simulant includes a 17.65 % increase to account for sodium additions in pretreatment [20, 55].
The nominal concentration, expressed in terms of the sodium molarity, was estimated on the
basis of melter feed rheology tests on similar formulations [56, 57]. The concentration of the
simulant used in melter tests was 8.0 molar sodium. This is higher than that used in previous
melter tests [32, 34] because of the higher waste loading in the ORP glasses. As the waste
loading increases, waste simulants at higher concentrations can be used to prepare melter feed
because lesser amounts of glass forming chemicals need to be added. The nominal simulant
formulation is given in Table 2.2. The LAW AN-107 simulant is a solution of predominantly
sodium, nitrate, nitrite, and sulfate.

 17

2.2 Glass Formulations

 Glass formulations for the two Hanford tank waste simulants for AN-105 and AN-107
were originally developed for the WTP baseline and extensively tested over a range of melter
scales. Subsequently, the waste loadings were increased slightly to allow for projected process
analytical variations and the formulations were tested on the DM10 and DM100 melters to
determine their tolerance for halides, chromium, and phosphate with respect to the formation of
secondary salt phases [12, 14, 15]. New glass formulations with considerably higher waste
loadings were later developed for these LAW streams [7-9]. The enhancements were achieved in
part by the use of vanadium, zirconium, and tin as additives at the expense of iron, titanium,
magnesium, and zinc. Chromium was also used as an additive to limit refractory corrosion from
the high alkali glasses. The WTP baseline and enhanced glass formulations and their respective
contributions from waste oxides are compared in Tables 2.3 and 2.4. The enhanced glass
formulations to be tested in the present work, ORPLA20 for the LAW AN-105 waste simulant
and ORPLB4 for the LAW AN-107 waste simulant, have been shown to have acceptable PCT
and VHT performance, levels of crystallinity, and processing properties and characteristics [7-9].

2.3 Feed Formulations for Melter Testing

 The types and amounts of glass former additives used to prepare the melter feed along
with the target feed properties are given in Table 2.5. The glass former additives are the same as
those planned for use at the WTP, with the exception of chromium, tin, and vanadium, which
would be new additives. The composition changes from one test to another were accommodated
by the omission of sufficient sodium hydroxide to allow for the sodium to be added back as
various combinations of the hydroxides, chlorides, and sulfates in order to achieve the desired
concentrations for individual tests while maintaining constant sodium concentration for each of
the two waste and associated glass compositions. The stock feed for the ORPLA20 composition
was batched at VSL and the stock feed for the ORPLB4 composition was batched by Optima
Chemicals. Both stock feed batches were analyzed to verify solids content and chemical
composition prior to use. Chlorine, sulfur, chromium, sugar, and a small portion of the sodium
were added to individual small batches for each test at VSL.

2.4 Sugar Additions to Feed

 With high nitrate feeds, the addition of reductants is necessary in order to control melt
foaming. Sugar, which was used for this purpose at West Valley, has also been selected as the
baseline reductant for the WTP. The amount of sugar required increases with the amount of
nitrates present in the feed and decreases with the amount of waste organics present in the feed,
which themselves act as reductants. Excessive additions of reductants can be deleterious, leading
to over-reduction of the melt and formation of sulfides and molten metals. Consequently, the
oxidants and reductants in the feed must be suitably balanced. The basis for achieving this
balance was developed by VSL and EnergySolutions for the vitrification of high-sodium-nitrate

 18

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

feeds at Savannah River's M-Area and has been successfully applied to the processing of a wide
variety of simulated WTP feeds over the past six years. In developing this approach, we elected
to conservatively adopt the most reducing potential reaction as the basis for the definition of a
"sugar” or stoichiometric ratio of 1.0 as a result of concerns for over-reducing the melt. Such a
reaction, using sodium salts as an example, is:

         C12H22O11 + 8NaNO3 = 8CO2 + 4CO + 4N2 + 11H2O + 4Na2O

 Fundamentally, the basis that is selected is simply a convention, since the precise
stoichiometry of the reactions involved is neither known nor constant under the conditions
prevailing in the melter. However, with this convention, a sugar ratio of 1.0 corresponds to one
mole of sucrose per eight moles of nitrate or, more generally, 1.5 moles of organic carbon per
mole of nitrate. It is then expected that significantly less sugar than this will be required in
practice. The empirically determined amount required to successfully control melt foaming
without significantly reducing the glass melt was found to correspond to a ratio of 0.5 when any
nitrites present were counted as nitrates (i.e., 0.75 moles of organic carbon per mole of nitrate +
nitrite). This approach has been employed for all WTP melter testing. It is, however, expected
that slight variations around the nominal value of 0.5 may be necessary to account for differences
in the reducing power of waste organics in comparison to sugar, particularly for LAW streams
that are high in organics.

 As an example, the calculation of the amount of sugar needed for the present LAW
AN-105 feed to achieve a sugar ratio of 0.5 proceeds as follows:

  One liter of 8 molar sodium simulant contains 1.857 moles of nitrite and 2.048
 moles of nitrate, giving a total of 3.905 moles of NOx (see Table 2.1)
  The required total amount of organic carbon for a sugar ratio of 0.5 is
                    3.905  0.75 = 2.929 moles
  One liter of simulant contains 0.174 moles of organic carbon (see Table 2.1)
  Therefore, 2.929 – 0.174 = 2.755 moles of organic carbon must be added.

 Since the molecular weight of sucrose is 342 g, 2.755  342/12 = 78.5 g sugar must be
added per liter of simulant, as shown in Table 2.5.

2.5 Analysis of Melter Feed Samples

 2.5.1 General Properties

 Feed samples from each drum of feed either received from the vendor (Optima) or
produced at VSL were analyzed to confirm physical properties and chemical composition.
Samples were taken from feeds prior to adjusting the sulfur, halide, chromium, and sodium
contents at VSL. Sample names, sampling dates, origin of the feed, and measured properties are

 19

provided in Table 2.6. The average measured glass yield for the feed samples was less than two
and quarter percent from the target values (on a mass per unit mass basis) validating the use of
the target values for calculating glass production rates. Measured feed densities, water contents,
feed solids, and pH are within narrow ranges for each feed composition sampled from different
drums, demonstrating the consistency of the feed used in the tests. The minor additions of
chlorine, sugar, chromium, and sodium are not expected to have any significant effect on the
measured feed properties.

 2.5.2 Chemical Composition

 The chemical compositions of the feed samples were determined by first making a glass
from the feed sample via crucible melt. The glass was subsequently crushed and analyzed
directly by X-Ray Fluorescence Spectroscopy (XRF). The boron concentrations were measured
by Direct Current Plasma - Atomic Emission Spectroscopy (DCP-AES) on dissolved glass
samples and were subsequently used for normalizing the XRF data. The analyzed compositions
of the feed samples are compared to the adjusted target compositions in Tables 2.7 and 2.8. The
data show good agreement with the modified target compositions for the major components and
most of the minor components. Measured boron concentrations were within two percent of the
target values, validating the use of the target values for normalizing the XRF data. The XRF
values had deviations of less than 10% for oxides with target concentrations of one percent or
greater. Absolute deviations for constituents targeted at lower concentrations were small except
for potassium in the ORPLAB4 formulation. Potassium oxide was measured at 0.47 wt% in the
ORPLAB4 feed as compared to the target of 0.12 wt%, while many of the WTP LAW glasses
(ORPLA20, ORPLA38-1, ORPLC5, ORPLE12, and ORPLF7) were targeted and tested at
around 0.5 wt% potassium oxide. A similar excess of potassium was observed in the previous
test with the ORPLAB4 formulation [7] suggesting low level contamination of chemicals or
additives unique to this low potassium feed composition. Surpluses of potassium may result in
the feed being slightly more likely to form secondary phases although the effect should be
minimal due to the low concentrations in the feed. Sulfur and chlorine were measured in the
feeds at low concentrations even though they had yet to be added intentionally to the feed.
Titanium oxide was detected in the feed samples at 0.05 to 0.29 weight percent, even though it
was not included in the target composition. Similar observations were made in previous tests
with LAW melter feeds [6-8, 16, 58] and is a result of its presence as a contaminant in the glass
forming additives, most notably kyanite [2]. Bismuth, manganese, nickel, phosphorus, lead, and
strontium were also found as trace level contaminants in the feeds. The low levels of
contamination are not expected to have an effect on secondary phases during the tests.

 Adjustments to the as-received or stock feeds were made to achieve the targeted sodium,
sulfur, chlorine, and chromium concentrations for each test. The feeds were procured without
sulfur and chlorine in the target compositions. The amount of chromium in the as-received feed
was due to the small amount present in the waste simulant and was subsequently augmented with
Cr2O3 as an additive. The targeted amount of 0.5 wt% Cr2O3 in the final glass composition was
reduced to 0.42 wt% to compensate for the above-target amount measured in the product glass
due to chromium originating from corrosion of the melter refractories, which tends to be higher

 20

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

for smaller melters as a result of surface to volume effects [16]. Sodium sulfate and sodium
chloride were added to achieve the higher target concentrations of chloride and sulfate. Sodium
hydroxide solutions were subsequently added to maintain the target sodium concentrations.
Water was then added to preserve the targeted feed solids contents.

 21

 SECTION 3.0

 EFFECT OF CHLORIDE AND GLASS FORMER ADDITIVES
 ON SALT FORMATION

3.1 Experimental Design and Test Methods

 3.1.1 General Approach

 A two tier approach was used for selection of glass former additives to mitigate salt
formation and to investigate the impact of chloride on sulfate salt formation. The candidate glass
former additives were first screened for their effect on sulfate solubility at a relatively high
chloride concentration. The test samples that accommodated higher sulfate concentrations were
identified and further investigated for the effect of chloride concentrations on sulfate loading.
The phase equilibria between the molten salt and molten glass were analyzed in order to
investigate the interaction between chloride, sulfate, and key glass components. The results from
a second set of tests formed the basis for selection of the formulation that accommodated the
highest sulfate and chloride concentrations without the formation of secondary phases. That glass
was further characterized with respect to key performance and processing properties.

 3.1.2 Over-Saturation Tests in Sealed Crucibles

 The solubility of sulfate and chloride in LAW glass melts was determined by analysis of
the glass in equilibrium with the molten salt at the test temperature. The starting materials for
each test contained chloride and sulfate in excess of their projected solubility. Thus, the
saturation of the glass melt was ensured by maintaining a stable molten salt phase containing
sulfur and chlorine in direct contact with the molten glass at the test temperature using a sealed
crucible arrangement. As shown in Figure 3.1, the mixture of molten salt and molten glass (five
grams) was contained in a Pt/Au crucible (5 ml) fitted with a Pt/Au lid, sealed within a larger
alumina crucible using nepheline ceramic paste that acted as a viscous sealant at the test
temperature [59]. The arrangement of a sample crucible inside a sealed confining crucible was
effective in preventing the volatilization of the molten salt phase. At the end of the saturation
tests, the samples were examined for the presence of a separated salt phase. The salt mixtures
collected contained both chloride and sulfate in addition to other components originating from
the starting glass compositions. The separated salt phase on glass surface and in the test vessel
was dissolving using 0.75% HNO3 solution for subsequent DCP/IC analysis. Mass balance
calculations were performed for each experiment to determine salt retention. The results are
presented in the subsequent sections.

 Previous work indicates that phase equilibrium between the molten salt and the molten
glass is attained in less than 2 hours at 1150oC, as demonstrated in numerous over-batching

 22

experiments for sulfate solubility [21, 22, 60]. To further confirm this, two sets of experiments
were conducted in the present work using sealed crucibles with different starting chemicals or
different test durations. The test conditions and chemical analysis of the final glass and salt
phases from these tests are listed in Table 3.1. Experiments with different starting materials, but
the same target composition (with the exception of a very minor difference in SiO2
concentration), were conducted at the same two-hour test duration. The results from tests using
Na2CO3 and Na2SiO3 (Tests 2 and 3, respectively) demonstrate that the chemical compositions of
the final glass and salt phase are virtually identical (Tables 3.2 and 3.3). In another comparison
test, identical mixtures of glass frit ORPLA20H and Fe2O3 additive (Tables 3.1, 3.2, and 3.3)
were melted for 2 and 4 hours. The analyses of the glass products and salt phases after the tests
are again almost identical (Tables 3.1, 3.2, and 3.3). It was thus concluded that the salt solubility
data from the sealed crucible setup at 1150oC are reproducible irrespective of starting materials
and that test durations of two hours was sufficient to reach phase equilibrium between the molten
salt and the glass melt. Therefore the concentrations of sulfate and chloride in the glass samples
should represent the solubility of those salt components in the presence of the coexisting salt
phase.

 All experimental data reported in this work (except for those specifically identified) were
obtained from sealed crucible tests of 4 hours duration. All glass samples were ground (minus
#200 sieve) and washed (with 0.75% HNO3) before submission for XRF analysis. All solutions
collected from washing the salt phase were analyzed by DCP/IC and the masses of the major salt
components were calculated from the measured concentrations and the total volume of solution.

 3.1.3 Chemical Composition of Glass/Salt Samples

 The compositions of the glasses were determined using X-ray fluorescence (XRF)
spectroscopy. The solutions collected from washing the salt phase were made up to a standard
volume and analyzed with direct current plasma atomic emission spectroscopy (DCP-AES) and
Ion Chromatography (IC), as described below.

 Powdered glass samples from the oversaturation experiments (–200 mesh) were analyzed
with a PANalytical AxiosmAX-Advanced XRF spectrometer. The spectrometer was calibrated
over a range of glass compositions using standard reference materials traceable to National
Institute of Standard and Technology (NIST) as well as a wide range of waste glasses. Analysis
by XRF provided data for most glass components of interest except lithium and boron, which
were reported as the target values.

 3.1.4 Characterization of Processing and Product Quality Properties

 3.1.4.1 Secondary Phase Evaluation

 LAW glass samples (about 5 grams each) were heat-treated in a platinum-gold crucible at
a pre-melt temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour, followed by heat treatment for 20 hours at 950,

 23

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

900, 850 and 800°C respectively. At the end of the heat-treatment period, the glass samples were
quenched by contacting the outer surface of the crucible with cold water. The sample was then
prepared for SEM/EDS examination to determine the volume fraction of crystalline phases and
for identification of the dominant crystalline phases.

 The crystalline phases found in the heat-treated glasses were characterized by SEM/EDS
and the volume percents were estimated by SEM image analysis. The heat treated samples were
processed by grinding and sieving (-18 mesh, < 1 mm) before SEM examination in order to
provide a representative measure of the overall crystal fraction in the sample.

 3.1.4.2 Product Consistency Test

 The product consistency test (PCT, ASTM C 1285) is used to evaluate the relative
chemical durability of glasses by measuring the concentrations of the chemical species released
from 100-200 mesh crushed glass (75-149 μm) to the test solution (de-ionized water in this case).
PCT tests were performed at 90ºC in accordance with the WTP contract requirement. The ratio
of the glass surface area to the solution volume for this test is about 2000 m-1 (typically, 4 g of
100-200 mesh glass is immersed in 40 ml of deionized water). All tests are conducted in
triplicate, in 304L stainless steel vessels, and in parallel with a standard glass included in each
test set. The internal standard is the Argonne National Laboratory Low Activity Waste Reference
Material (ANL-LRM) glass [61] and/or the DWPF-Environmental Assessment (EA) glass [62],
both of which have undergone round-robin testing. The leachates are sampled at seven days. One
milliliter of sampled leachate is mixed with 20 ml of 1M HNO3 and the resulting solution is
analyzed by DCP-AES; another 3 ml of sampled leachate is used for pH measurement.

 3.1.4.3 Electrical Conductivity

 The electrical conductivity (σ) of the glass melt was determined by measuring the
resistance of the melt as a function of frequency using a calibrated platinum/rhodium electrode
probe attached to a Hewlett-Packard model 4194A impedance analyzer. Measurements were
performed at temperatures (T) around 950, 1050, 1150 and 1250ºC. The results were analyzed
using an equivalent circuit model to obtain the DC electrical conductivity. To facilitate
comparison, the electrical conductivity data were interpolated to standard temperatures (e.g.,
1150ºC) using the Vogel-Fulcher equation:

                                             ln σ = [A/(T–To)] + B                                           (4.2)

where A, B, and To are fitting parameters. Estimated uncertainties in the electrical conductivity
measurements are ±20 relative%.

 24

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

         3.1.4.4            Viscosity

 The melt viscosity (η) of each glass was measured using a Brookfield viscometer with a
platinum-rhodium spindle and crucible. The relative torque of a rotating spindle immersed in
molten glass was measured as a function of rotational velocity (revolutions per minute (RPM)) at
four temperatures (T) in the range of 950-1250ºC, similar to those employed for electrical
conductivity. To facilitate comparison, the viscosity data were then interpolated to standard
temperatures (e.g., 1150ºC) using the Vogel-Fulcher equation:

                                            ln η = [A/(T–To)] + B,                                        (4.3)

where A, B, and To are fitting parameters. The equipment was calibrated at room temperature
using standard oils of known viscosity and then checked in the temperature range of 950 to
1250ºC using a National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) standard reference glass
(SRM 711). Both precision and accuracy of the viscosity measurements are estimated to be
within ±15 relative %.

 3.1.4.5 Vapor Hydration Test

 The vapor hydration test was run in Parr series 4700 screw-cap pressure vessels made of
304L stainless steel and having either 22 or 45 ml capacity, in accordance with the corresponding
VSL procedure. The glass coupon was cut and polished, dimensional measurements were made
to permit calculation of the area, and the coupon was weighed before and after the VHT on a
balance having a resolution of 100 g. The coupon was suspended from a hanger in the pressure
vessel and enough de-ionized water was added to the vessel to saturate the volume at the test
temperature of 200ºC and to allow for a non-dripping layer covering the coupon. The pressure
vessel was sealed, weighed, and placed in an oven held at 200ºC for 24 days. The temperature
was monitored continuously with an independent thermocouple. At the completion of the test,
the pressure vessel was removed and immediately partially immersed in an ice/water bath to
condense the water vapor near the bottom of the vessel. Once cool, the vessel was weighed and
opened, then the coupon removed and weighed, and subsequently examined using low-power
optical microscopy. The cross section of the reacted VHT coupon was analyzed by SEM/EDS to
determine the thickness of the altered layer.

 3.1.4.6 Refractory Corrosion

 Corrosion of the K-3 glass contact refractory in the glass melt was measured using a
modified ASTM refractory corrosion procedure (ASTM C 621-84) [63]. In this test, the K-3 test
coupon, with dimensions of 0.4 x 0.6 x 3 inch, was partially immersed in 170 grams of the LAW
test glass melt at 1208oC in a 200 ml platinum crucible for six days with continuous air bubbling.
At the end of the corrosion test, the K-3 coupon was removed from the melt and sectioned
lengthwise for measurement of dimensional changes. Per ASTM C-621-84, the dimension losses

 25

at the “neck” (the glass-air interface) and the “half-down” (half of the immersed length of the
coupon below the neck) locations are reported.

 3.1.4.7 Feed Conversion by Vertical Gradient Furnace (VGF) Testing

 Observations of feed conversion using Vertical Gradient Furnace tests provide a useful
screening assessment of the melt rate of the feed [64]. The temperature gradient inside the VGF
is maintained by two separate sets of heating elements, both of which are arranged in cylindrical
form and aligned along their axes. The inner heater is set at 1150oC, which is the nominal
temperature of the glass pool, and the ambient heater is set at 600oC, which is similar to the
melter plenum temperature. A ceramic crucible (4 inches tall) is used to contain the reacting
melter feed. The temperature inside the test crucible and its gradient are measured with an S-type
thermocouple at different vertical locations and maintained by adjusting the inner heater
temperature. For a typical feed conversion test, 10 grams of glass of identical chemical
composition to the test feed (expressed on an oxide basis) is preheated in the ceramic crucible
positioned in the inner heater before the dried melter feed (to yield about 20 grams of glass) is
introduced. Feed reactions under the controlled temperature gradient are allowed to continue for
the designated test duration (typically for 30 and 60 minutes, but this may vary with feed type)
and then stopped by rapid cooling in room temperature air. The top surface of the reacted feed
material is then inspected and photographed. The crucibles with their feed contents are then
cross-sectioned to reveal the conversion progress of feed blends. The saw cuts of the crucibles
were performed dry (without lubricant) to avoid loss of any soluble material. To characterize the
reacted feed material, visual inspection and digital imaging of the top (by photography) and cross
section (using an optical scanner) of the reacted sample were performed.

3.2 Results from Screening Tests

 Glass ORPLA20, based on LAW from Tank AN-105, was selected as the base glass for
screening additives for their potential to suppress salt formation at high sulfate and chloride
concentrations. ORPLA20 was reformulated to introduce 5 wt% of additives at the expense of
MgO, Fe2O3, SnO2 and part of the CaO. In addition, NaCl and Na2SO4 were included in
sufficient quantities to ensure oversaturation in the presence of a molten salt phase at 1150oC.
The reformulated glass frit (before mixing with additives and salt components) is identified in
Table 3.4 as ORPLA20H without Cl but with 0.38 wt% SO3. The ORPLA20H frit was used to
test the additives CoO, CuO, Fe2O3, Fe3O4, MnO2, NiO, SnO2, and V2O5 (Table 3.5). In view of
the relatively high concentration of ZrO2 already in ORPLA20H, ZrO2, which was originally
proposed as one of the potential additives, was replaced by P2O5 in this work. Because of the
highly hydroscopic nature of P2O5, frit ORPLA20HP with 5 wt% P2O5 was prepared for the
screening test with P2O5 (Table 3.5). Table 3.6 provides the batching recipes for screening, and
subsequent tests. Na2SiO3 and SiO2 were used to compensate the bulk composition changes in
Na2O and SiO2 that would result from the NaCl, Na2SO4, and Na2SiO3 additions. Na2CO3 was
not used after the initial exploratory test (Test 2) due to its tendency to induce foaming as
carbonate decomposes.

 26

 A complete list of the salt oversaturation experiments is given in Table 3.5. The
experimental details of tests with NiO, CoO MnO/MnO2, SnO2, Fe3O4, Fe2O3, CuO/Cu2O, V2O5,
and P2O5, along with the Cl/SO3 variations, are provided in Tables 3.5 - 3.8. The results from the
screening tests for each additive are summarized below.

 3.2.1 NiO

 At the high Cl content of 0.65 wt%, 0.39 wt% SO3 was present in ORPLA20HCl-Ni glass
 o
at 1150 C along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium sulfate and chloride. A small
amount of potassium chromate also was present in the salt phase along with a minor amount of
calcium. Mass balance calculations on the basis of the batching recipe and analysis of the
product glass and salt indicated about 91 - 95% retention of Cl and SO3 after 4-hour sealed
crucible tests (Table 3.5).

 3.2.2 CoO

 The first test using a mixture of ORPLA20H glass frit and CoO powder plus other salt
components failed due to excessive foaming. The experiment was therefore conducted in two
steps. During step one the CoO and the ORPLA20 frit were mixed and melted in a larger Pt
crucible in open air to facilitate the equilibration of CoO at the test temperature. In step two, the
melt thus conditioned at 1150oC was mixed with additional salt components and loaded into a
sealed crucible for the salt saturation test. Using this two step process, the test with CoO was
completed without deleterious foaming. At the high Cl content of 0.66 wt%, 0.35 wt% SO3 was
present in ORPLA20HCl-Co glass at 1150oC along with a separate salt layer of predominantly
sodium sulfate and chloride. A small amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase
along with a minor amount of calcium. Mass balance calculations based on the batching recipe
and analysis of the product glass and salt indicated about 88% retention of Cl and virtually
complete retention of SO3 after 4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5).

         3.2.3     MnO2/MnO

 The first test with the mixture of ORPLA20H glass frit and MnO2 powder plus other salt
components failed due to excessive foaming. The experiment was repeated by replacing MnO2
with MnO at otherwise identical conditions. However, that test also failed due to foaming.
Consequently, the mixture of ORPLA20H and MnO was pre-melted in a larger Pt crucible at
1150oC and quenched into glass before being used for the salt oversaturation experiment in a two
steps procedure similar to that adopted for the test with CoO. However, this test also exhibited
foaming and therefore no further tests were attempted with MnO2/MnO.

 27

 3.2.4 SnO2

 Only part of the 5 wt% SnO2 added to the batch dissolved in the glass frit at 1150oC. A
substantial amount of undissolved SnO2 formed a solid layer on the bottom of the crucible, as
confirmed by SEM (Figure 3.2). The glass contained 0.65 wt% Cl and 0.39 wt% SO3 with a
separate salt layer similar to that observed in the test with NiO, but with less Ca. Mass balance
calculations based on the batching recipe and analysis of the product glass and salt indicated
retention of about 77% of Cl and 88% of SO3 after 4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5).

         3.2.5 Fe3O4

 At the high Cl content of 0.63 wt%, 0.36 wt% SO3 was present in ORPLA20HCl-Fe3O4
glass at 1150oC along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium sulfate and chloride. A
small amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase along with a minor amount of
calcium. Mass balance calculations based on the batching recipe and analysis of the product
glass and salt indicated retention of about 82% of Cl and 92% of SO3 after 4-hour sealed crucible
tests (Table 3.5). Analysis of the product glass using Mossbauer spectroscopy indicated that
essentially all of the ferrous iron introduced from Fe3O4 (1/3 of the iron is ferrous) was oxidized
with only about 1.7% of Fe2+ remaining after 4-hour sealed crucible tests, which is comparable
to the typical iron redox in melts performed in ambient air.

         3.2.6 Fe2O3

 At the high Cl content of 0.64 wt%, 0.38 wt% SO3 was present in ORPLA20HCl-Fe2O3
glass at 1150oC along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium sulfate and chloride. A
small amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase along with a minor amount of
calcium. The concentrations of Cl and SO3 in both glass and salt were very similar to those in the
test with Fe3O4. Mass balance calculations based on the batching recipe and analysis of the
product glass and salt indicated about 91% retention for Cl and complete retention for SO3 after
4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5). Analysis of the glass sample using Mossbauer
spectroscopy indicated 2.4% Fe2+ remaining after 4-hour sealed crucible tests; this can be
compared to the value of 3.4% Fe2+ obtained in a 2-hour test with Fe2O3 and the value of 1.7%
Fe2+ obtained in a 4-hour test with Fe3O4.

         3.2.7 CuO/Cu2O

 The test using a mixture of ORPLA20H glass frit and CuO powder plus other salt
components failed due to excessive foaming. The foaming was likely caused by reduction of
CuO at the test temperature while CuO was being dissolved into the molten glass. A more
reduced form, Cu2O, was therefore used in place of CuO in a subsequent test; no foaming
occurred in that test. At the high Cl content of 0.68 wt%, 0.43 wt% SO3 was present in
ORPLA20HCl-Cu glass at 1150oC along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium
sulfate and chloride. A small amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase along

 28

with a minor amount of calcium. Mass balance calculations based on the batching recipe and
analysis of the product glass and salt indicated about 83% retention of Cl and virtually complete
retention of SO3 after 4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5).

 3.2.8 V2O5

 The experimental details of V2O5-added Cl/SO3 saturation test are documented in Tables
3.5 - 3.8. At the high Cl content of 0.79 wt%, 0.60 wt% SO3 was present in ORPLA20HCl-V
glass at 1150oC along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium sulfate and chloride. A
small amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase along with a minor amount of
calcium. In addition, the salt phase contained substantial amounts of vanadate (comparable to
chloride in normality). Mass balance calculations based on the batching recipe and analysis of
the product glass and salt indicated about 76% retention of Cl and about 79% retention of SO3
after 4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5).

 3.2.9 P2O5

 At the high Cl content of 0.65 wt%, 0.38 wt% SO3 was present in ORPLA20HCl-P glass
 o
at 1150 C along with a separate salt layer of predominantly sodium sulfate and chloride. A small
amount of potassium chromate was present in the salt phase along with a minor amount of
calcium. In addition, the salt phase contained substantial amounts of phosphate (more than
chloride in normality). Mass balance calculations on the basis of batching recipe and analysis of
the product glass and salt indicated about 84% retention of Cl and about 88% retention of SO3
after 4-hour sealed crucible tests (Table 3.5).

 3.2.10 Evaluation of Screening Tests

 Overall, the volatilization loss of Cl and SO3 from the test setup was controlled, with
greater than 90 wt% SO3 and 80 wt% Cl retentions in the majority of the samples from the
screening tests (Table 3.5). The test with V2O5 showed the highest loss of about 20 wt% for SO3
and 24 wt% for Cl, which is consistent with observations from melter tests [4]. Nevertheless, a
separate salt phase was still maintained at the end of the oversaturation test with V2O5 and
enough salt was collected for DCP/IC analysis. As shown in Figure 3.3, most of the glass
samples contained about 0.35 to 0.4 wt% SO3 and from 0.63 to 0.66 wt% Cl. Glass from the test
with Cu2O contained slightly higher SO3 and Cl contents at 0.44 wt% and 0.68 wt%,
respectively. In contrast, glass from the test with V2O5 contained 0.6 wt% SO3 and nearly 0.8
wt% Cl, both of which are substantially above the typical solubility values in the screening
group. Consequently, V2O5 and Cu2O were selected for further investigation of sulfate solubility
at various chloride concentrations. Considering the significant presence of P2O5 in Hanford
LAW and the beneficial effect of P2O5 in the mitigation of sulfate salt formation observed in
melter tests [14, 15, 25, 26], P2O5 was also included in subsequent tests.

 29

3.3 Sulfate Solubility at Different Chloride Concentrations

 Glass samples with V2O5, Cu2O, and P2O5 additives were selected for further tests of
sulfate solubility at different concentrations of chloride. The experimental conditions and recipes
for the mixtures of glass frit and oxide/salt additives are given in Table 3.6 for mid and zero Cl
contents. The Cl/SO3 oversaturation experiments were conducted using the same sealed crucible
arrangement at 1150oC for 4 hours as used for the screening tests. The glass and salt samples
were prepared and analyzed as described for the screening tests. The results are summarized
below.

 3.3.1 Cu2O Additive at Different Cl Concentrations

 The salt oversaturation experiments with Cu2O at mid and zero levels of chloride resulted
in similar retention as in the screening test at high chloride concentration (Table 3.5). Salt phases
were present on the surface of the samples after both tests. SO3 dissolved in the glass phase
increased steadily with decreasing Cl concentration in the glass (Tables 3.5 - 3.8, Figure 3.4). In
accordance with the lower total chloride content in the mixture, the chloride concentration in the
salt phase decreased from about 30 normality% of the anion group in the high chloride test, to
about 13 normality% in the mid chloride test, to zero for the Cl-free test, and was largely
compensated by the sulfate anion, while other salt components changed little (Tables 3.7 and
3.9).

 3.3.2 V2O5 Additive at Different Cl Concentrations

 The salt oversaturation experiments with V2O5 at mid and zero levels of chloride resulted
in similar retention as in the screening test at high chloride concentration (Table 3.5). Salt phases
were present on the surface of the final glass samples after both tests. SO3 dissolved in the glass
phase increased steadily with decreasing Cl concentration in the glass (Tables 3.5 - 3.8, Figure
3.4). In accordance with the lower total chloride content in the mixture, the chloride
concentration in the salt phase decreased from about 25 normality% in the anion group for the
high chloride test, to about 12 normality% for the mid chloride test, to zero for the Cl-free test,
and was largely compensated by the sulfate anion, while other salt components changed little
(Tables 3.7 and 3.9).

 3.3.3 P2O5 Additive at Different Cl Concentrations

 The salt oversaturation experiments with P2O5 at mid and zero levels of chloride resulted
in similar retention as in the screening test at high chloride concentration (Table 3.5). Salt phases
were present on the surface of the final glass samples after both tests. SO3 dissolved in the glass
phase increased steadily with decreasing Cl concentrations in the glass (Tables 3.6 - 3.8, Figure

 30

3.4). In accordance with the lower total chloride content in the mixture, the chloride
concentration in the salt phase decreased from about 24 normality% in the anion group for the
high chloride test, to about 13 normality% for the mid chloride test, to zero for the Cl-free test,
and was largely compensated by the sulfate anion, while other salt components changed little
(Tables 3.7 and 3.9).

 3.3.4 Baseline ORPLA20H Frit at Different Cl Concentrations

 Tests with P2O5 and V2O5 showed that these additives were present in both the glass and
salt phases. In order to provide a baseline free of influence from the various additives, a salt
oversaturation test was conducted with the ORPLA20H base glass without any additives. Table
3.6c provides the batching recipes for baseline frit tests at high, mid, and zero chloride contents.
The salt oversaturation experiments with ORPLA20H frit resulted in similar SO3 and Cl
retention as in other tests (for example, three tests with Cu2O at high, mid, and zero chloride
(Tables 3.5, 3.7, and 3.8)). Salt phases were present on the surface of the samples after all three
tests. SO3 dissolved in the glass phase increased steadily with decreasing Cl concentration in the
glass (Table 3.5, Figure 3.4). In accordance with the lower total chloride content in the mixture,
the chloride concentration in the salt phase decreased from about 31 normality% in the anion
group for the high chloride test, to about 12 normality% for the mid chloride test, to zero for the
Cl-free test, and was largely compensated by sulfate anion, while other salt components changed
little (Tables 3.7 and 3.9).

 3.3.5 Sulfate Solubility and Its Dependence on Chloride Concentration

 Sulfate solubility in all four glass samples with different additives (or without additive for
the baseline frit) increases with decreasing chloride concentration in a consistent manner. A
linear trend is evident for each additive tested (Figure 3.4). This negative correlation is most
apparent for the glass with V2O5. As shown in Figure 3.4, SO3 in the glass increased by 40%
from about 0.6 wt% at the highest Cl content to 0.84 wt% in the Cl-free sample. In comparison,
the SO3 solubility increased by 24% and 16% for glasses with Cu2O and P2O5 additives,
respectively. The trend defined by the three ORPLA20H glass samples more or less parallels that
for Cu2O. At all three Cl concentrations, SO3 solubility is consistently the highest in the glass
containing V2O5, followed by the baseline frit ORPLA20H, then the Cu2O additive, and lowest
with P2O5. Therefore, at all Cl concentrations tested in the glass/salt mixture, V2O5 was most
effective in facilitating the dissolution of SO3, followed by the baseline frit, Cu2O, and P2O5. The
results can also be viewed from the perspective of the effect of SO3 on the chloride concentration
in the glass, which is generally very steep but less so with the V2O5 additive.

 As shown in Figure 3.5, for each group of samples loaded with the same amount of NaCl
in the mixture, the variations in the Cl concentration in the glass tend to increase from the lowest
to the highest added Cl level.

 31

 3.3.6 SO3/Cl Solubility in LAW Glass Melt Versus Molten Salt Composition

 In analyzing the data from these tests, it must be recognized that the sulfate/chloride
loading in the glass melt depends not only on the glass composition but also on the composition
of the salt phase. Since the molten salt is actually a solution of anions and cations, whose
concentrations varied with the additives, the solubility of sulfate and chloride in the glass melt is
determined by the chemical potential of the corresponding salt components in the molten salt
phase as well as the solution chemistry in molten glass.

 The results from chemical analysis of the major salt components are listed in Table 3.7
after conversion to their respective masses collected at the end of the oversaturation tests. The
elements identified by DCP/IC were further divided into anion and cation groups, and their
relative ion normality% within each group was calculated. As listed in Table 3.9, S, Cl, Cr, V
and P were treated as anions in the forms of SO42-, Cl-, CrO42-, VO43- and PO43-, respectively,
while Na, K, and Ca were treated as cations in the forms of Na+, K+ and Ca2+. The relative
concentrations of the salt phases are given in Table 3.9 with each anion and cation expressed in
normality% (counted in terms of moles of unit charge) with respect to the anion or cation group,
respectively. Salt samples with additives of Fe2O3, Fe3O4, NiO, SnO2, CoO and Cu2O (at high Cl
content) are very similar, with around 60 normality% sulfate anion, around 30 normality% Cl
anion, and less than 10 normality% Cr anion, while the cations are predominantly Na, with less
than 10 normality% of K and Ca. In contrast, the salt samples from tests with V2O5 or P2O5
additives at the high chloride contents contained considerable amounts of vanadate or phosphate.
For example, the anion group from the test with V2O5 contained 22 normality% vanadate anion,
while the anion group from the test with P2O5 contained 31 normality% phosphate anion. As a
result of the partitioning of V or P into the molten salt phases, the chemical potential of sulfate or
chloride, as estimated by their respective anion normality% in the molten salt phase, decreased in
comparison to the salt phases of the other tests, which would inevitably impact the solubility of
SO3 or Cl in the glass. Therefore, the capacity of a glass melt to accommodate salt components
should be evaluated from both perspectives, i.e., from the concentrations of SO3 and Cl in the
glass melts and also from the compositions of the coexisting salt phases. More specifically, the
capacity to accommodate salt components needs to be compared on a common basis that is not
biased by the composition variations in the molten salt phase, as described below.

 Figure 3.6 shows the trend of SO3 wt% in glass (sulfate solubility in the presence of the
corresponding salt phase) versus SO42- normality% in the salt phase (which is related to the
chemical potential of SO42- imposed by the equilibrium molten salt phase). In a simple system in
which the chemical potential of sulfate in the salt phase is equal to its sulfate normality% [65]
and the concentration of SO3 dissolved in glass melt follows Henry’s law for a dilute solution,
the sulfate concentration in the glass increases linearly with increasing normality of sulfate in the
salt phase. This behavior is observed for the present result in Figure 3.6 where the slope of the
trend line is the reciprocal of Henry’s constant. It is interesting to note that almost all data points
fall closely to a common trend line with the exception of the three samples with V2O5, which fall
on a trend line with nearly double the slope. In other words, at the same sulfate chemical
potential in the salt phase, glass melts with 5 wt% V2O5 can dissolve nearly two times as much
SO3 as the glasses with various other additives or the baseline glass frit.

 32

 It is also apparent from Figure 3.6 that the trend line defined by the three tests with Cu2O
additive is similar to that defined by the three tests with P2O5 additives. The results for the three
tests with the baseline ORPLA20H frit without additives also fall close to the trend line for the
tests with Cu2O and P2O5 at different levels of Cl as well as the results from the screening tests
with the rest of the additives, which were conducted only at the high Cl level. Thus, additives
other than V2O5 that were tested had similar effects on the solution behavior of sulfate species in
the LAW glass melts and changes in these additives did not significantly alter the Henry’s Law
constant. Nevertheless, SO3 solubility decreased as P2O5 was added to the base glass because of
decrease in the chemical potential of sulfate in the molten salt phase as a result of the
participation of phosphate in the molten salt; thus, these data points are displaced towards the
origin. Less SO3 was dissolved in the glass with P2O5 because the sulfate anion normality% in
this case was the lowest among all additives tested at high chloride content. The common trend
line followed by tests using the baseline frit and P and Cu additives suggests that the lower
sulfate solubility observed in glass with P2O5 requires careful analysis: one cannot conclude that
P2O5 negatively impacts the sulfate solubility since the dilution by P2O5 in the salt phase was
responsible for the observed lower SO3 wt% in glasses containing P2O5. In other words, the glass
melt contains less SO3 simply because it is in equilibrium with a molten salt phase in which the
chemical potential of SO3 is lower.

 The observed dilution effect is obviously dependent on the total amount of salt with
respect to the glass, which was specific to conditions selected for the sealed crucible tests.
However, extrapolation of the trend line of SO3 wt% in glass versus sulfate species normality%
in the salt phase provides a means of comparison that is independent of these case-specific
factors. This is shown in Figure 3.7, which suggests that the maximum SO3 loading in ORPLA20
glass without vanadium is likely to be below 0.7 wt% (the value obtained by extrapolation to 100
normality%, i.e., a pure sulfate salt phase). A similar extrapolation shows that the maximum
amount of SO3 that can be accommodated by ORPLA20 with 5 wt% V2O5 is about 1.2 wt%,
which represents a substantial increase.

 In summary, while both V2O5 and P2O5 are present in significant amounts in the salt
phase, which, by dilution, tends to decrease the sulfate activity and therefore also its
concentration in the equilibrium glass melt, V2O5 also decreases the activity coefficient of sulfate
in the glass melt (as indicated by the significantly higher Henry’s constant (Figure 3.6)), which
tends to increase its concentration, and this latter effect overwhelms the former.

 In contrast to the sulfate solubility data, the Cl solubility results with various additives
showed only a single trend line, as shown in Figure 3.8. In contrast to its very large effect on
sulfate solubility, the effect of V2O5 on chloride solubility was similar to that for P2O5, Cu2O, or
simply the baseline glass frit, all of which showed slightly higher Cl concentrations than the rest
of the additives tested in the screening experiments. Similar to sulfate solubility, the solubility of
Cl also appears to obey Henry’s law but with no apparent distinction between the Henry’s
constant with the various additives; there is also considerable scatter in the data points at high Cl
contents for the various additives and some indication of developing curvature in the trend.

 33

 3.3.7 Summary of Results and Recommendation of Additive

 The salt oversaturation tests with the modified high sodium ORPLA20 glass identified
V2O5 as the most effective additive for mitigation of salt formation. At zero, mid, and high
concentrations of chloride (Cl from 0, 0.4, and 0.7 wt% in the glass melts), glass melts with
5 wt% V2O5 dissolved from 0.6 wt% (high-Cl glass) to more than 0.8 wt% SO3 (Cl-free glass) in
comparison to roughly 0.4 wt% (high-Cl glass) to 0.6 wt% SO3 (Cl-free glass) for the rest of the
group. More importantly, sulfate solubility when compared at equal sulfate chemical potential in
the salt is approximately 75% higher in melts with V2O5 as compared to melts with other
additives. The maximum amount of SO3 that can be accommodated by ORPLA20 with 5 wt%
V2O5 is estimated at 1.2 wt% (extrapolated from the trend line assuming the chemical potential
for pure sodium sulfate) as compared to about 0.7 wt% for those without V2O5 (Figure 3.7).

 The presence of chloride in ORPLA20 glass strongly affected the solubility of sulfate.
The amount of sulfate dissolved in all four glasses tested (with V2O5, P2O5, and Cu2O, plus the
base glass frit) displayed a downward trend with increasing Cl wt% in the glass melt due to the
combined effects of the reduced sulfate chemical potential in the salt phase and the interaction
between Cl and SO3 in the glass melt. The decrease of SO3 wt% in the glass is a joint effect due
to decrease in sulfate chemical potential in the salt phase and increases in Cl wt% in the glass
phase and additional tests are needed to fully resolve the effects of each of these factors.

 Although not directly comparable, it is of interest nevertheless to compare the measured
effect of chloride on sulfate salt formation from these crucible tests with the corresponding
effects determined in continuous melter tests from previous work [16] and the present work (see
Section 6). Figure 3.9 shows such a comparison. It should be noted that the sealed crucible data
relate to two specific glass compositions and to the concentrations measured in the glass. In
contrast, the melter trends relate to the concentrations in the feed and are based on data for many
glasses with different compositions, some with and some without vanadium, various sodium
concentrations, etc. The potassium content of the crucible glass is essentially the same as that for
the glasses used to develop the low-potassium melter data trend; on that basis, therefore, the
crucible data should be compared to the low-potassium melter trend. With these caveats in mind,
significant and potentially important differences are evident. Interestingly, the effect of chlorine
on sulfate salt formation is more pronounced (larger negative slope in Figure 3.9) for the melter
data than for the crucible data. As a result, at low chlorine contents the crucible data fall below
the melter data, as might be expected based on the equilibration with the coexisting salt phase in
the crucible tests, but that is evidently not the case at higher chloride concentrations. Further
work would be useful in order to better understand these differences.

 On the basis of the results from the above tests, V2O5 was identified as the most effective
glass additive to enhance sulfate and chloride loadings. These results are consistent with earlier
findings with respect to sulfate salt formation [25, 26, 66-70], which led to the use of V2O5 in
many of the high waste loading LAW glass formulations that were developed at VSL for ORP.
Accordingly, a modified version of ORPLA20 with 5 wt% V2O5 was selected for further
characterization of properties relevant to processing and product quality.

 34

3.4 Characterization of Glass with V2O5 Additive

 Table 3.4 lists the chemical composition of the modified ORPLA20 glass with 5 wt%
V2O5. The results from characterization of the corresponding glass (ORPLA20HV) and melter
feed are summarized below.

 3.4.1 Characterization of Glass ORPLA20HV

 Results from XRF analysis of glass ORPLA20HV are listed together with the target glass
composition in Table 3.4. Glass ORPLA20HV was melted and quenched into a homogeneous
and transparent glass typical of high sodium LAW glasses. Glass ORPLA20HV showed high but
marginally acceptable leaching resistance by PCT (Table 3.10) and 24-day VHT alteration
(Figure 3.10); ORPLA20HV is considerably less durable than ORPLA20 based on the results
from both PCT and VHT. The viscosity and electrical conductivity (Table 3.11) of the
ORPLA20HV glass melt are within the WTP limits for LAW glass melts [71]. Glass
ORPLA20HV was free of any crystallization after 20-hour isothermal heat treatment at 950, 900,
850, and 800oC. Material compatibility tests with K3 refractory showed minimal dimensional
loss after a 6-day corrosion test with continuous air bubbling (Figure 3.11, Table 3.12), which
was virtually identical to that obtained for ORPLA20 [8]. Overall, the glass ORPLA20HV meets
all WTP processing and product quality requirements. The leaching performance of
ORPLA20HV, which was not optimized in this work, can potentially be improved through
composition refinement in the future.

 3.4.2 Characterization of Melter Feed Slurry of ORPLA20HV

 To evaluate the possible impact of the high V2O5 addition on feed processing rate in a
melter, a feed sample of the ORPLA20H formulation with V2O5 was prepared using AN-105
LAW simulant and glass forming chemicals typical for LAW feeds. The melt rate of this feed
sample was evaluated using a VGF test following a procedure developed previously [64]. The
top views and cross sections of the partially reacted feed samples after 15, 30, and 60 min VGF
tests are given in Figure 3.12; the results from XRF analysis of the fused feed material are given
in Table 3.13. As described in the earlier work [64], the degree of feed conversion in the VGF
test can be correlated to the feed processing rate from melter tests. The assessment of the feed
conversion of slurry sample ORPLA20HV indicates that this feed has characteristics similar to
those of fast-processing HLW melter feed samples. However, signs of foaming were visible in
the samples from VGF tests. Foaming likely developed and dissipated rapidly during the early
stage of feed reaction, as suggested by the 15-minute VGF test result. Earlier work [64] showed
that a dome-like structure can form as the result of foaming, which can slow down feed
conversion during melter processing. Although no dome structure was present, a considerable
amount of foamy material accumulated near the rim of the test crucibles from the VGF test
samples at shorter durations, probably as a result of a short-lived foaming event. However, the

 35

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

foamy, partially-reacted feed material reacted rapidly afterwards so that only minor amounts
remained after the 60-minute VGF test. The rapid foaming of this LAW feed may have been
caused by reduction of V2O5 during melting. However, more systematic tests are needed to
identify the cause of the observed foaming.

 In summary, the visual evaluation of VGF test results suggests a fast processing rate for
