the ORPLA20HV formulation with V2O5.

 36

 SECTION 4.0
 DM10 TESTS

 Melter tests were conducted on the DM10 with the LAW simulants from 11/13/13 to
1/8/14 to determine the maximum chloride concentration that can be processed without forming
secondary phases for two glass compositions each at three target sulfur contents. These tests
produced over 400 kg of glass from about 800 kg of feed. Tables 4.1 and 4.2 provide summaries
of the DM10 tests, including run times, the amount of chlorine and sulfur in the feed, the amount
of feed processed, the amount of feed chlorine and sulfur retained in the glass product,
observations of secondary phases, and key processing parameters. The tests, listed in the order in
which they were performed, were as follows:

   Test 5: Nine nominally 14-hour feeding segments with LAW AN-105 simulants with a
 Na2O concentration of 24 wt% in the ORPLA20 target glass composition. The test
       segments tested concentrations of 1.06 and 1.26 wt% Cl at 0.41 wt% SO3; 0.66, 0.86, 1.06
       and 1.26 wt% Cl at 0.56 wt% SO3; and 0.66, 0.86, and 1.06 wt% Cl at 0.70 wt% SO3 in the
 glass product (assuming total retention).

   Test 6: Eight nominally 14-hour feeding segments with LAW AN-107 simulants with a
 Na2O concentration of 24 wt% in the ORPLB4 target glass composition. The test segments
       tested concentrations of 0.71, 0.91 and 1.11 wt% Cl at 0.46 wt% SO3; 0.31, 0.51 and 0.71
 wt% Cl at 0.66 wt% SO3; and 0.11 and 0.31 wt% Cl at 0.85 wt% SO3 in the glass product
 (assuming total retention).

 The principal objective of these tests was to determine, for each feed, the maximum
amount of chlorine that can be present in the melter feed without forming secondary sulfate
phases; this was done at three sulfur concentrations for each of the two different glass
compositions. In all tests, the bubbling rate was adjusted to maintain target glass production rates
between 2250 and 2500 kg/m2/day with a complete cold cap. Test segment durations of 12 to 14
hours were selected since, at the target glass production rate, this provided three melt pool
turnovers (24 kg) for each feed composition. Sugar was added to the feed at a stoichiometric
carbon ratio of 0.5 for the melter tests. At the end of each test segment, dip samples were taken
to detect the presence of a separate sulfur phase on the glass pool surface. The melt surface was
considered free of a sulfate layer if no visible secondary sulfate phases were observed on any of
the three dip samples. If a sulfate layer was detected on the melt surface, the glass pool was
bubbled until the dip samples indicated that the sulfate layer had dissipated prior to commencing
the subsequent test segment.

 37

4.1 DM10 System Description

 4.1.1 Feed System

 The feed container is mounted on a load cell for weight monitoring and is stirred
continuously except for periodic, momentary interruptions during which the weight is recorded.
The material in the feed container is constantly recirculated, which provides additional mixing.
The recirculation loop extends to the top of the melter where feed is diverted from the
recirculation loop through a peristaltic pump into the melter through a Teflon-lined feed line and
vertical water-cooled feed tube. A diverter valve permits direction of the feed stream either to the
melter or to a sampling vessel.

         4.1.2     Melter

 The DM10 system used for this work is a ceramic refractory lined melter, which includes
two Inconel 690 plate electrodes that are used for joule-heating of the glass pool and a bubbler
for agitating the melt. Glass is discharged from the melter using an air-lift system. The melt pool
has a surface area of 0.021 m2 and typically contains about 8 kg of glass. The plenum volume is
19.5 liters at the nominal glass level. Inconel 690 thermowells were custom fabricated and
installed in the DM10 for the present tests since in previous tests, thermowells made from
Inconel 601 experienced rapid corrosion in tests with high-alkali, high-sulfur feeds [5].

 4.1.3 Off-Gas System

 For operational simplicity, the DM10 is equipped with a dry off-gas treatment system
involving gas filtration operations only. Exhaust gases leave the melter plenum through a film
cooler device that minimizes the formation of solid deposits. The film cooler air has constant
flow rate and its temperature is thermostatically controlled. The geometry of the transition line
(between the melter and the first filtration device) conforms to the requirements of the
40-CFR-60 air sampling techniques. Immediately downstream of the transition line are cyclonic
filters followed by conventional pre-filters and High-Efficiency Particulate Air (HEPA) filters.
The temperature of the cyclonic filters is maintained above 150oC while the HEPAs are held
above 100oC to prevent moisture condensation. The entire train of gas filtration operations is
duplicated and each train is used alternately. An induced draft fan completes the system. The
sampling location for gaseous species monitored by Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy
(FTIR) is immediately downstream of the fan.

4.2 DM10 Test Conditions

 Target processing conditions, including bubbling rate adjusted to maintain a target
production rate between 2250 and 2500 kg/m2/day, a melt pool temperature of 1150°C, and a
complete cold cap were achieved throughout the majority of the melter tests. The melter was

 38

idled twice for repairs during these tests: the first (3 hours 15 minutes) during Test 5A to replace
cabling to the east electrode and the second (1 hour 10 minutes) during Test 6D to correct for
over-feeding. There was no indication that the need to replace the electrode cable during Test 5A
was a direct consequence of the tests but rather the cumulative effect of many years of melter use
and idling. Prior to each test series, sufficient feed of each composition was processed to achieve
the target composition in the melt pool. The last test segment with the ORPLA20 composition
(5F) was conducted after the test series with the ORPLB4. The composition of the melt pool was
changed back to the ORPLA20 composition by feeding a sufficient amount of glass produced
from earlier tests with this composition. Test segment average production rates were within ten
percent of the target range for all but the last test conducted, which was only marginally below
the ten percent criterion (1928 kg/m2/day).

 Temperatures monitored throughout the melter system during the tests illustrate that
target test conditions were achieved during testing. The target glass temperature of 1150°C was
achieved for the majority of testing. The measured test segment average glass temperatures two
and four inches from the melt pool floor were between 1140 - 1155°C for all test segments,
except for two segments (6G and 5F) in which high electrode temperatures limited glass
temperatures to 1124 to 1138°C. Measured glass temperatures at the two locations in the glass
pool were within 4°C of each other throughout the tests indicating a lack of a temperature
gradient throughout the bulk of the glass pool. Over the first nine tests, the glass temperature was
up to 70°C higher than that of the electrodes while in the last eight tests the electrodes were up to
90°C hotter than the glass. In an effort to limit the increasing electrode temperatures during Tests
6G and 5F, the amount of power applied was limited, resulting in glass temperatures below the
target range. Typical plots of DM10 melter temperatures are given in Figures 4.1.a and 4.1.b for
the first four test segments with the ORPLA20 composition (5G, 5H, 5J, and 5D) and five test
segments with the ORPLB4 composition (6D, 6E, 6F, 6G, and 6A). Variations in temperatures
measured at both locations were observed during the idling periods used to take glass dip
samples and purge the melt surface of secondary sulfate phases. The difference in temperature
between the two electrodes was variable over the course of the tests; the East electrode was
hotter than the West electrode in five tests, colder in eleven tests, and the same in one test. The
measured plenum temperatures were below 600°C for the majority of the tests, indicating that a
complete cold cap covered the melt pool surface throughout the tests. Exceptions were tests
conducted in 2014 in which plenum temperatures were about 50°C higher in response to the
higher electrode temperatures. Typical plots of DM10 plenum temperatures are given in Figures
4.2.a and 4.2.b for the first four test segments with the ORPLA20 composition (5G, 5H, 5J, and
5D) and five test segments with the ORPLB4 composition (6D, 6E, 6F, 6G, and 6A). Plenum
temperatures were higher at the beginning of each test, decreased to around 550°C as the cold
cap developed, then rose in between test segments while the melt pool was sampled for
secondary sulfate phases. Unlike plenum temperature measurements on larger melters, the
exposed thermocouple often gave a lower temperature reading than the thermocouple in the
thermowell due to variable amounts of feed coating the exposed thermocouple.

 Since the tests were run at a constant glass production rate, the ease of processing each
feed is reflected in the amount of bubbling required to maintain that rate. Test segment average
bubbling rates ranged from 1.5 to 3.7 and 2.9 to 3.8 liters per minute for the ORPLA20 and

 39

ORPLB4 compositions, respectively, reflecting the relative ease observed in previous tests with
these compositions [7,8]. Typical plots of DM10 melter bubbling rates are given in Figures 4.3.a
and 4.3.b for the first four test segments with the ORPLA20 composition (5G, 5H, 5J, and 5D)
and five test segments with the ORPLB4 composition (6D, 6E, 6F, 6G, and 6A). Bubbling rates
were decreased to minimal levels during sampling of the melt pool at the end of each test
segment and then increased to facilitate the removal of secondary phases on the surface of the
melt pool.

 Power supplied to the electrodes typically averaged between 6 and 7.5 kW for most of the
test segments. Overall, the power demand while processing the ORPLA20 composition was
about 0.5 kW lower than while processing the ORPLB4 composition. Typical plots of power
supplied to the melter and resistance of the glass melt are given in Figures 4.4.a and 4.4.b for the
first four test segments with the ORPLA20 composition (5G, 5H, 5J, and 5D) and five test
segments with the ORPLB4 composition (6D, 6E, 6F, 6G, and 6A). The glass resistance
generally ranged between 0.08 and 0.11 ohms, which is typical of high alkali glass compositions.
Momentary decreases in power and corresponding increases in glass resistance were observed
while discharging glass in tests following the repair of the cable leading to the electrode during
Test 5A.

4.3 DM10 Test Results

 Depictions of the target and measured sulfur and chlorine contents are provided in
Figures 4.5 and 4.6 for the test series. Results for each of the compositions can be summarized as
follows:

   Test 5: LAW AN-105 simulant with a Na2O concentration of 24 wt% in the ORPLA20
 target glass composition. During tests with the lowest tested SO3 target glass concentration
 of 0.41 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while processing feeds targeted at
 1.26 wt% Cl but not at 1.06 wt% Cl. Thus, at the target sulfur concentration of 0.41 wt%
 SO3, between 1.06 and 1.26 wt% Cl with respect to the feed can be tolerated without the
 formation of secondary phases. During tests with the intermediate SO3 target glass
 concentration of 0.56 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while processing feeds
 targeted at 1.26 wt% Cl but not at 0.66, 0.86, and 1.06 wt% Cl. Thus, at the target sulfur
 concentration of 0.56 wt% SO3, between 1.06 and 1.26 wt% Cl in the feed can be tolerated
 without the formation of secondary phases. During tests with highest tested SO3 target
 glass concentration of 0.7 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while processing
 feeds targeted at 1.06 wt% Cl but not at 0.66 and 0.86 wt% Cl. Thus, at the target sulfur
 concentration of 0.7 wt% SO3, between 0.86 and 1.06 wt% Cl with respect to the feed can
 be tolerated without the formation of secondary phases. The highest concentration
 combinations of sulfur and chlorine in the glass that did not result in the formation of
 secondary phases were: 0.38 wt% SO3 with 0.63 wt% Cl; 0.45 wt% SO3 with 0.60 wt% Cl;
 and 0.63 wt% SO3 with 0.49 wt% Cl. The highest measured sulfur concentration of 0.63
 wt% SO3 is the same measured concentration in glass that did not result in the formation of
 secondary phases in previous tests with the same glass formulation [8].

 40

   Test 6: LAW AN-107 simulant with a Na2O concentration of 24 wt% in the ORPLB4
 target glass composition. During tests with the lowest tested SO3 target glass concentration
 of 0.46 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while processing feeds targeted at
 1.11 wt% Cl but not at 0.71 and 0.91 wt% Cl. Thus, at the target sulfur concentration of
 0.46 wt% SO3, between 0.91 and 1.11 wt% Cl with respect to the feed can be tolerated
 without the formation of secondary phases. During tests with the intermediate SO3 target
 glass concentration of 0.66 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while processing
 feeds targeted at 0.71 wt% Cl but not at 0.31 and 0.51 wt% Cl. Thus, at the target sulfur
 concentration of 0.66 wt% SO3, between 0.51 and 0.71 wt% Cl with respect to the feed can
 be tolerated without the formation of secondary phases. During tests with highest tested
 SO3 target glass concentration of 0.85 wt%, secondary sulfate phases were observed while
 processing feeds targeted at 0.31 wt% Cl but not at the nominal 0.11 wt% Cl. Thus, at the
 target sulfur concentration of 0.85 wt% SO3, between 0.11 and 0.31 wt% Cl with respect to
 the feed can be tolerated without the formation of secondary phases. The highest
 concentration combinations of sulfur and chlorine in the glass that did not result in the
 formation of secondary phases were: 0.42 wt% SO3 with 0.43 wt% Cl; 0.60 wt% SO3 with
 0.31 wt% Cl; and 0.69 wt% SO3 with 0.11 wt% Cl. The highest measured sulfur
 concentration is lower (0.69 vs. 0.81wt% SO3) than the measured concentration in glass
 that did not result in the formation of secondary phases in previous tests with the same
 glass formulation [7].

 The relative volatility of sulfur and chlorine is illustrated in Figures 4.5 and 4.6 as the
difference between the target and measured amounts in the glass. From 77 to 94% and from 80 to
100% of the feed sulfur was retained in the glass for the ORPLA20 and ORPLB4 compositions,
respectively, indicating a relatively low sulfur volatility at these feed concentrations. These
results are consistent with previous testing which has demonstrated that only the low soda, high
sulfur LAW glasses volatilize a higher portion of the feed sulfur when vitrified at nominal WTP
conditions [4-6, 14-16, 25-36]. Chlorine loss varied between very little at the lowest chlorine
target concentrations to about half at the highest chlorine target concentrations. This observation
is largely consistent with the near-50% loss of chlorine from the glass at target concentrations
greater than 0.3 weight percent in previous tests [7, 15, 16, 34]. The higher chlorine retention at
the lower chlorine target values was also observed in previous tests with these wastes [6, 8, 9,
16], which may be due in part to the effects of the presence of chlorine as a contaminant in the
raw materials. More extensive sampling and analysis of the melter exhaust, which typically is
conducted for tests performed on larger melters [2-6, 14, 15, 25-36, 72-74], is required for a
more complete assessment of elemental volatility and mass balance calculations.

4.4 Gases Monitored by FTIR

 Melter emissions were monitored in each test for a variety of gaseous components, most
notably carbon monoxide, ammonia, water, and nitrogen species, by Fourier Transform Infra
Red Spectroscopy (FTIR). The off-gas system temperature is maintained well above 100°C
beyond the sampling port downstream of the HEPA filter in order to prevent analyte loss due to

 41

condensation prior to monitoring. Test segment average concentrations and measured ranges of
volatile species, including NO, NO2, CO, and NH3, are provided in Tables 4.3 and 4.4; these
analytes are those that were expected to be observed during the tests based on previous work.
Typical plots of melter exhaust concentrations of NO, NO2, CO, and NH3 are given in Figures
4.7.a - 4.9.b. The plots are for the first four test segments with the ORPLA20 composition (5G,
5H, 5J, and 5D) and five test segments with the ORPLB4 composition (6D, 6E, 6F, 6G, and 6A).
Little or no HF, HCl, HCN, SO2, nitrous acid, or nitric acid was detected in any of the tests. The
most abundant nitrogen species monitored was NO, which is consistent with previous tests [2-9,
14-16, 25-36] in which nitrates and nitrites were abundant in the feed. The carbon monoxide and
ammonia observed in melter emissions as products of incomplete combustion for LAW
simulants are typical while processing relatively high concentrations of nitrate, nitrite, and sugar.
The relative concentration of the nitrogen oxide and carbon monoxide is dependent on the feed
composition (nitrates, nitrites, organic reductants) and the feed rate. Since the feed rate for each
waste composition was intentionally similar, the concentrations of nitrogen oxides and carbon
monoxide are relatively constant over each test series. The two LAW simulants have similar
nitrate and nitrite contents and therefore also have similar monitored nitrogen oxide
concentrations in the exhaust. Variations of nitrogen oxides and carbon monoxide concentrations
of up to 50% were observed throughout each test in response to the dynamic nature of the
processes occurring in the cold cap.

 42

 SECTION 5.0
 DM10 GLASS PRODUCTS

 Over four hundred kilograms of glass was produced in these tests. The glass was
discharged from the melter periodically into square steel cans using an airlift system. The
discharged product glass was sampled at the end of each test by removing sufficient glass from
the top of the cans for total inorganic analysis. Care was exercised during sampling of each can
to identify and segregate any potential secondary phases. Minor amounts of secondary sulfate
were observed on four of the one hundred and ten sampled glasses, mostly on the exterior of
drips from the end of discharge. Representative photographs of these secondary phases are
shown in Figures 5.1-5.3. These occurrences of secondary phases were transient and had no
correlation with the presence or absence of secondary phases on the glass pool surface. Product
glass masses, discharge date, and analyses performed are listed in Table 5.1. Glass samples were
also obtained by dipping a rod into the glass pool at the beginning and end of each test. These
"dip samples" underwent visual examination to detect the presence of a separate sulfate phase on
the glass pool surface.

5.1 Compositional Analysis

 Glass discharge samples were crushed and analyzed directly by XRF. The target values
for boron and lithium oxides, which are not determined by XRF, were used for normalizing the
XRF data to 100 wt%. The XRF-analyzed compositions of all discharged glass samples are
provided in Tables 5.2 and 5.3. A comparison of average measured glass compositions to the
nominal target compositions is provided in Table 5.4. The majority of the XRF analysis results
compare favorably to their corresponding target values and feed sample analysis (see Section
2.5.2). Of the oxides with target concentrations of one percent or greater, the XRF values had
deviations of less than 8%. Measured potassium oxide concentrations were on target for the
higher concentration of 0.53 wt% in the ORPLA20 composition but exceeded the low target of
0.11 wt% in the ORPLB4 composition. Chromium oxide concentrations were up to an absolute
0.12 wt% above the target as a result of corrosion of melter refractory and Inconel components
despite the reduction of chromium in the feed to sixteen percent below target levels. Similarly,
chromium concentrations in product glasses were also above the target concentrations in the
preceding tests after the same reductions in feed chromium [16]. Elements not included in the
target glass compositions, including bismuth, manganese, nickel, phosphorus, and titanium, were
observed in the product analysis as a result of either corrosion of melter components, carryover
from previous tests, or trace contamination in the additives. Similarly, iron oxide, which was
targeted at less than one weight percent, was also over-represented in the glass product by an
absolute 0.18 wt% for both compositions.

 Compositional trends of the major and select oxides during the tests are shown in
Figures 5.4 - 5.10. These plots illustrate the differences between the two tested compositions and

 43

the closeness to the respective targets over the course of the tests. Aside from the intended
changes in sulfur and chlorine, the glass compositions were held relatively constant while testing
each glass composition. Silica, soda, and alumina, which constitute about three quarters of each
glass composition, change very little over the course of testing, as intended. Other trends
observed in the tabular data are apparent in the graphical depictions. There was excellent
agreement with the target composition throughout testing for oxides of Ca, Mg, Sn, V, Zn, and
Zr. Also evident are the surpluses of chromium and iron of up to 0.2 wt% oxide in response to
corrosion of melter components or low level contamination of the feed. Higher surpluses are
observed after extended idling periods in between tests, further indicating the corrosion of melter
components as the source of the surplus chromium and, to a lesser extent, iron. The potassium
concentrations do not appreciably decrease from the 0.5 wt% oxide target of the ORPLA20
composition while processing the ORPLB4, which has a target concentration of only 0.11 wt%
oxide.

5.2 Secondary Phase Observations

 All discharged glass and glass “dip” samples taken directly from the melt pool were
closely examined to document the presence or absence of secondary phases. Glass dip samples
were obtained from three separate locations in the melt pool at the end of each test segment;
these samples were collected to ascertain whether a secondary sulfate layer had formed on the
surface of the glass melt in response to the feed sulfur and chlorine concentrations used for that
test segment. Samples were also taken to ensure that the melt surface was free of secondary
phases prior to starting each test segment, as well as after bubbling that was intended to volatilize
sulfur from a previously formed sulfur layer. Table 5.5 provides a listing of all of the dip samples
and whether or not a separate salt phase was evident. Examples of secondary phases observed
while processing the formulations are shown in Figures 5.11 - 5.15. Notice the yellow material
adhering to the rod and on the outside of the glass, both of which are indicative of a sample taken
from a melt pool with a sulfate layer on the surface. Select dip glass samples that were free of
secondary phases were analyzed by XRF to determine their chemical compositions. The
analyzed compositions are compared to target compositions in Table 5.6. The analyzed
compositions compare well with the target compositions for most analytes in most glasses. The
measured sulfur concentrations are very similar to those measured in contemporaneous
discharged glasses shown in Tables 5.2 and 5.3.

5.3 Analysis of Secondary Deposits

 Samples of deposits that were collected from various locations throughout the melter
system were analyzed by XRF and DCP to determine their chemical composition. A description
of the samples, sampling date, and analyzed compositions are provided in Table 5.7. Film cooler,
transition line, and cyclone solids are an aggregate of material from current and preceding tests.
For example, the cyclone solids collected at the end of Test 5D also contain bismuth from
processing HLW simulants [59] and vanadium from processing LAW feeds with vanadium as an
additive [16] in preceding tests. Photographs of the transition line, film cooler, and cyclone filter

 44

prior to cleaning is provided in Figures 5.16 - 5.18. Notice the lighter color of the deposits
suggesting that much of the material is alkali salts as opposed to transition metals such as iron.
These observations are confirmed by the chemical analysis, which indicates that the deposits are
primarily sodium chloride, borate, and sulfate salts. Lesser amounts of non-volatile feed
constituents including silica, alumina and zirconia are present as a result of entrainment and
carryover into the exhaust. The composition of the deposits collected at the three locations are
very similar suggesting little segregation throughout this section of the off-gas system; the only
exception is a lower proportion of volatile salts depositing in the transition line.

 45

 SECTION 6.0
 DISCUSSION

6.1 Secondary Phases During Melter Tests

 The deleterious effects of molten salt formation are well-recognized and include impacts
on melter lifetime and operations as well as on product quality. Since the WTP has no means of
detecting the formation of molten salt phases, the mitigation strategy involves testing to
determine the conditions under which such phases form, development of appropriate
composition controls, and implementation of those composition controls during operations to
avoid salt phase formation. The LAW glass formulation correlation [12] provided the first step in
the development of such a compositional control scheme for the operation of the WTP LAW
vitrification system. Melter tests that were conducted to support the validation of the LAW
correlation that was developed for BNI [12] clearly demonstrated several trends with respect to
the formation of secondary phases while processing LAW wastes [14, 15]. Firstly, glass
formulations generated by the LAW glass formulation algorithm (including process uncertainties
in the worst-case direction) that span a wide range of sodium and sulfate concentrations can be
processed without forming separate sulfur phases on the melt surface. However, salt phase
formation can result when these formulations are employed for wastes with elevated levels of
chromium or halides within the ranges based on WTP flow-sheet projections. The results showed
that chlorine facilitated the formation of secondary phases to a much greater extent than fluorine,
although fluorine alone promoted secondary phase formation for some compositions [14, 15].
The addition of chromium facilitated the formation of secondary sulfur phases in both
formulations tested, as indicated by the decrease in halide concentrations that were processed in
DM10 tests without the formation of secondary sulfur phases, significant concentrations of
chromium measured in soluble secondary phases, and the formation of a secondary sulfur phase
during a DM100 test at nominal (0.2 Cl and 0.08 F wt%) halide concentrations [14]. The
addition of phosphate to the melter feed partially reduced the tendency of chromium to facilitate
the formation of secondary sulfur phases. Differences in the ability of the base glass
compositions to tolerate feed halides ranged from 0.2 wt% Cl and 0.19 wt% F in the LAWE7H
composition to 1.0 wt% Cl and 1.2 wt% F in the LAWE4H composition.

 The effect of chromium was exacerbated by potassium and mitigated by phosphorus, an
effect that had been found in previous tests [25]. With high chromium and potassium and low
phosphorus, even the nominal chlorine and fluorine concentrations led to salt formation in the
DM100 test. A further complication with respect to chromium is the fact that the melter
refractories and Inconel components provide additional sources of chromium that may affect the
tendency for salt phase formation.

 The test results [14, 15] also showed that, for glasses formulated from the LAW glass
formulation correlation with the nominal levels of chromium, phosphorus, and potassium, the
highest tolerable levels of chlorine and fluorine are strongly and non-linearly dependent on the

 46

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

sodium content 1. The tolerable level dropped by more than a factor of two on going from 5.72 to
13.53 wt% Na2O and then increased by more than a factor of five on going from 13.53 to
21.27 wt% Na2O. Finally, even in the absence of potassium, the addition of chromium brought
about a three-fold reduction in the tolerable level of halides.

 In the first [16] and second phase [17] of the present work, 46 tests were performed on
the DM10 melter system to determine the concentration of chloride that results in the formation
of a secondary sulfate phase on the glass pool surface for each of the four tested glass
compositions at various sulfur contents. The tests were conducted at nominal WTP LAW
processing conditions: 1150C glass temperature, glass production rate of 2000 -
 2250 kg/m2/day, complete cold cap, and sugar additions to the feed corresponding to a
stoichiometric ratio of 0.5 (1 mole sucrose per 16 moles NOx). The testing demonstrated the
impact of chlorine on sulfate phase formation in the alkali-limited region, the region in which
waste loading is limited by sulfate, and for the highest anticipated concentrations of both
chromium and phosphate. Higher waste loading glass compositions developed for the LAW
AP-101 (ORPLG27), AN-105 (ORPLA38-1 and ORPLA20), AN-102 (ORPLD6), AN-107
(ORPLB4), and AZ-101 (ORPLE12) tank compositions were evaluated. For each waste
composition and sulfur content, the chloride concentration was progressively increased to the
point at which a sulfate salt phase develops, indicating the limit of sulfate incorporation for that
particular formulation. The halide tolerance for each waste composition based on these tests is
summarized in Tables 6.1 and 6.2 for Phases 1 [16] and 2 [17] of the work, respectively. For
most of the waste and feed formulations tested the amount of chlorine that could be processed
without the formation of secondary phases decreased with increasing sulfur content in the feed.
The addition of sodium chromate facilitated the formation of secondary sulfur phases in both
formulations tested, as indicated by the appearance of secondary phases in tests that did not have
secondary sulfur phases without the additional chromium. Conversely, the addition of
phosphorus to the melter feed mitigated secondary phase formation in feeds that had previously
been processed with secondary sulfur phases. The addition of phosphate was not sufficient to
mitigate the formation of secondary sulfur phases in feeds with excess sodium chromate.

6.2 Correlations Obtained from Phase 1 Data

 This section describes correlations of the effects of sulfate and chloride on salt formation
from previous work [16] that were obtained from analysis of data from the Phase 1 tests on four
high waste loading glass formulations developed for the LAW from tanks AP-101 (ORPLG27),
AN-105 (ORPLA38-1), AN-102 (ORPLD6) and AZ-101 (ORPLE12) together with data from
previous tests [7-9] on these four formulations. These data are combined and plotted in Figure
6.1; note that the data for tests with increased phosphate or chromate are excluded from this plot,
which therefore relates only to the nominal values of these variables. The data fall along two

1
  Within the LAW glass formulation correlation, the glass formulation is, to a very large extent, determined by the
sodium content. Thus, while other components also vary as the sodium content changes, the variations are strongly
(and deliberately) correlated. Consequently, the net observed effect of halide tolerance is in reality due to the
combination of all of these composition changes.

 47

The Catholic University of America Melter Tests to Define LAW Halide Concentrations, Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-14R3280-1, Rev. 0

trends, one for the low-potassium formulations (~0.5 wt% K2O) and the other for the high-
potassium formulation (~5.7 wt% K2O). There is a clear and substantial offset between these
trends, illustrating the significant effect of potassium. The trend lines are intended to provide a
reasonable separation between compositions that lead to salt formation from those that do not.
The trend lines are given by:

                            Cl , wt %  1.65  1.1 * SO3 , wt %                   Low Potassium                     (1)
 and

                            Cl , wt %  1.45  2.1 * SO3 , wt %                   High Potassium                    (2)

The similarity of the intercepts suggests that, near zero sulfate content, higher potassium has only
a modest effect on salt formation at high chloride content. However, at higher sulfate contents,
the sensitivity of sulfate salt formation to chloride is nearly doubled at the higher potassium
content. The higher sulfate regime is more relevant to most of the WTP LAW feeds. If a simple
linear behavior with potassium content is assumed, the potassium dependence can be included by
interpolating between Equations (1) and (2), giving:

                            Cl , wt %  Cl L (1   )  Cl H  ,

where ClL and ClH are the values given by Equations (1) and (2), respectively, and

 (K  K L )
  ,
 (K H  K L )

where K is the potassium oxide content in question, KL is the mean potassium oxide content for
the data underlying Equation (1) (0.52 wt%), and KH is the potassium oxide content for the data
underlying Equation (1) (5.74 wt%), all in wt%.

 These trends serve to illustrate the relative effects of sulfate and chloride on salt
formation in these high waste loading glass formulations. However, as noted above, these simple
trends will shift with variations in phosphate and chromate, as well as with other glass
constituents. In addition, several tests with LAW feeds have demonstrated that both the tendency
to form secondary phases and the amount of feed sulfur retained in the glass is affected by the
type and amount of reductant in the feed [4, 25, 33]. Similarly, the amount of rhenium,
technetium, and iodine in melter feed is strongly influenced by oxidation state in the cold cap
and therefore the relative amount of oxidants and reductants in the feed [73-75]. The LAWE7H
composition was one most prone to the formation of secondary phases in response to halides [14,
15] and also had minimal retention of iodine and technetium as compared to other compositions
[73, 74]. This composition also has a high waste organic content, which is less effective than
sugar as a reductant, resulting in more oxidizing conditions in the cold cap when the total organic
content is fixed to the feed nitrate content (see Section 2.4).

 48

 Furthermore, while the facilitation of secondary phase formation by chromium is well
established, the different sources of chromium may behave differently in the cold cap and may
therefore have different effects on the formation of secondary phases. Chromium in the glass is
derived from four different sources: sodium chromate (Na2CrO4.4H2O) simulating the form of
chromium in LAW stream; chromium oxide (Cr2O3) used as an additive in the high waste
loading glass formulations to limit refractory corrosion; chromium leached from the refractories
and Inconel into the glass; and chromium present as a contaminant in additives and chemicals
used to make the waste simulant. The more oxidized hexavalent form of chromium in the waste
very likely plays a greater role in secondary phase formation than the trivalent chromium that is
used as an additive. For example, glasses formulated with the AN-102 waste containing
chromium as an additive (ORPD6) had higher halide and sulfur contents without the formation
of a secondary phase than a glass formulated without chromium as an additive (LAWE7H).
Chromium leached from the refractories directly into the glass should be much less involved in
the reactions in the cold cap where secondary phases form; therefore it would be expected to
have a lesser effect on the formation of secondary phases than would chromium in the feed. The
role of chromium originating as a feed contaminant is even more difficult to determine given the
variable amount and unknown species; up to 0.1 wt% Cr2O3 has been measured in calcined feed
and this contribution is not always negligible. Testing designed to distinguish the differing
effects of the various forms of chromium on secondary phase formation would be useful in
defining limits for the amounts and types of chromium that can be treated and for formulating
glass compositions that do not contribute to secondary phase formation.

6.3 Incorporation of Phase 2 Data

 Phase 2 work added data from 17 tests on two high waste loading glass formulations
developed for the LAW from tanks AN-105 (ORPLA20) and AN-107 (ORPLB4). All of these
tests were conducted with the respective nominal levels of chromium, phosphorus, and fluorine
since these species were not deliberately varied in these tests. In addition, all of the tests relate to
the low-potassium regime (~0.5 wt% K2O) described in Section 6.2. The new data were
therefore added to the low-potassium data set plotted in Figure 6.1 and compared to the
corresponding correlation expressed by Equation (1) as shown in Figure 6.2. The new data show
generally good consistency with the low-potassium correlation defined by the Phase 1 data;
significant exceptions are the data for tests 6B and 6F, which would be predicted to be salt-free
but did show a salt phase. A review of the data showed no anomalies in these tests and although
the salt phase was detected on only two out of three dip samples for both tests, the salt was
clearly present. These tests are two of the eight tests with the highest fluorine concentrations
(target of 0.47 wt% versus 0 - 0.2 wt% for the rest of the tests), which may account for part of
the discrepancy; however, previous work has suggested that the effect of fluorine is significantly
smaller than that of chlorine [14, 15]. Additional testing would be useful to verify these data
points and better define the effect of fluorine on salt formation.

 49

6.4 Combined Baseline Plus High Waste Loading Dataset

 One of the objectives of the present tests was to expand the data set to provide an
improved basis for the development of models for predicting salt formation. The combined data
generated from the BNI baseline glass compositions [14, 15], the high waste loading LAW glass
compositions [5-9], and from the present two phases of testing [16, 17] consist of 147 tests
conducted on the DM10 and DM100 with seven waste compositions and twenty-one different
glass compositions. The compositional ranges of the feeds processed on a calcined basis are as
follows: 5.72 - 24 wt% Na2O; 0 - 5.74 wt% K2O; 0.2 - 1.625 wt% SO3; 0.01 - 1.23 wt% Cl;
0 - 1.47 wt% F; 0.03 - 1.0 wt% Cr2O3; and 0 - 3.24% P2O5. It should be noted that the present
tests were conducted on high waste loading glass formulations that were developed for ORP
whereas the data generated during the correlation validation melter tests [14, 15] were for the
BNI baseline glass compositions. As a result, analysis of the full data set will require
consideration of the potentially significant effects of other glass composition variables besides
sulfate, halides, phosphate, and chromate. Nevertheless, testing on both types of glass
formulations has clearly shown that increasing the concentrations of sulfur, chloride, chromium,
and to a lesser degree fluorine, increases the likelihood of secondary phase formation for each
glass formulation.

 Figures 6.3 and 6.4 display the occurrence of salt formation for the combined data set
with respect to the chlorine, sulfur, and sodium plus potassium oxide contents. The data are
distinguished in terms of tests without secondary phases (“pass”), with secondary phases (“fail”),
from BNI baseline glasses [14, 15], and from high waste loading glass compositions [5-9, 16,
present tests]. Feed target values are used except for many of the potassium values, which had
analyzed feed compositions that deviated from the target values. For each alkali or sulfur
content, tests without secondary phases have lower collective concentrations of sulfur and
halides for each unique glass formulation. However, at least in these projections, there is no
simple division between tests that do and do not form secondary phases, which is consistent with
the fact that other constituents contribute significantly to the tendency to form secondary phases.
The contributions of chlorine, sulfur, and chromium towards the formation of secondary phases
is illustrated in Figure 6.5, which shows the cumulative number of tests with secondary phases
with respect to feed chlorine and sulfur content and glass chromium content. The trends are quite
similar with respect to concentrations of the three individual constituents.

6.5 Previously Proposed Limits and Rules for Salt Formation

 An initial attempt to augment the LAW glass formulation correlation to include the effect
of halides [13] used the data generated during the correlation validation melter tests [14, 15] and
data from the LAW Pilot Melter in Columbia MD. Data from the Bechtel Correlation Tests are
plotted according to the prescribed protocol in Figure 6.6. The LAW Pilot Melter data are not
included due to the low halide concentrations in those feeds. Six data points that were previously
erroneously denoted as free of secondary phases [13] and one erroneous fluorine concentration
[13] were also corrected. About as many tests without secondary phases (“pass”) are on either
side of the proposed limit, which is not effective in separating test with salt from those without.

 50

Some of the failures below the limit are ascribed to higher concentrations of potassium and
chromium, which, as discussed above, have previously been identified as facilitating the
formation of secondary phases.

 Subsequently, two more complex schemes have been proposed that attempt to include the
effects of sulfate, halides, chromium, potassium, and phosphate on salt formation [18, 19]. These
two sets of rules for limiting salt formation are listed Table 6.3. Each of these two schemes was
then compared to the applicable data for the tests that conform to each rule. Differences in the
data set used in the previous modeling efforts [18, 19] and the data presented in the present
report are as follows:

  Obviously, the data from the present tests were not included in previous models.
  Six data points that were erroneously denoted as free of secondary phases [18, 19] were
 replaced with the correct information from the source report [15].
  Analyzed feed concentrations of potassium, chromium, and phosphate were used here
 instead of the respective target values to account for contamination frequently observed
 with these species.
  The LAW Pilot Melter data were not included here due to the low halide concentrations
 in those feeds and the lack of any test data indicating the presence of secondary phases.
  Both DM10 and DM100 test data from early ORP tests [5, 6] are included here while
 mostly only DM10 data were used in the previous modeling efforts [18, 19].

 The first set of limits [13, 18] was based on a simple relationship between halide and
sulfur concentrations in the melter feed (Rules 5 and 6; Table 6.3) as well as limits for
chromium, phosphate, and potassium in the feed based on the target values tested at VSL (Rules
7, 8, 9; Table 6.3). Table 6.3 also summarizes the conformance of the test data to these rules.
Comparison of the test data to Rules 5 and 6 is illustrated in Figure 6.7. The majority of tests
processed feeds with sulfur and halide concentrations above the stated limit, as shown in Figure
6.7. About half of the tests conform to the proposed limits but in general the rules are not
effective in differentiating between compositions that do and do not form salt. The few tests that
fall below the limits (predicted to be salt free) were at the low halide concentrations but salt
phases were observed in several of those tests. About one-third to one-half of the tests do not
conform to the proposed rules for phosphate, chromium, and potassium. This is in part due to the
fact that many of the tests have higher than target concentrations of these constituents but the
rules were based on the target values. Perhaps more importantly, the data set includes limited
information on the limits of chromium and potassium that can be processed without the
formation of secondary phases.

 The second set of limits, termed the “conservative halide rule” [19], is based on a
relationship between halides, chromium, potassium, and sulfur concentrations in the melter feed.
Conformance of the test data to this rule is summarized in Table 6.3 and illustrated in Figure 6.8.
About one-third to one-half of the tests do not conform to the prediction based on this rule.

 51

Again, as with the first scheme discussed above, most of the tests have concentrations placing
the values above the limits indicating the limit is overly conservative with respect to the
variables evaluated. Essentially, this rule simply predicts that the vast majority of the tests in the
dataset should have formed salt, which was not the case. The addition of potassium to the term
for the vertical axis shifts all of the tests that were conducted with K2O concentrations above
about 1.5 wt% up to a factor of two to three above the limit. As shown in Figure 6.8, many of
these tests did not form salt despite the fact that they fall far above the limit. This suggests that
the proposed limit is overly conservative with respect to potassium. In summary, the
“conservative halide rule” would exclude the processing of all but the lowest concentrations of
halide, potassium, and chromium, whereas there is an abundance of feed compositions above the
limit that have been processed without salt formation.

 Another proposed limit is termed the “optimistic halide rule” [19]. This rule, which is
also listed in Table 6.3, is based on a relationship between halides and chromium in the feed and
sulfur concentrations measured in the product glass. The conformance of the test data to this rule
is illustrated in Figure 6.9. All tests that did not form a salt phase fall below this limit, but so too
do nearly all of those that did form a salt phase. Thus, this rule is essentially just the upper
envelope of all of the tests that have been conducted and predicts that no tests within that
envelope should form salt. Thus, this rule seems overly optimistic based on the available data.
Furthermore, using this rule to differentiate between feed compositions that can be processed
without salt formation is challenging given that the rule is based upon the analyzed glass product
as opposed to the measured melter feed concentrations.

 The rules discussed above were used for recent estimations of the mass of LAW glass
that is projected to be produced by vitrification of all of the Hanford LAW under various
scenarios [76].

 In summary, none of the proposed rules are effective in clearly separating compositions
that form salt from those that do not and they would therefore not be appropriate for controlling
WTP operations. This is due in part to the still rather limited data set in comparison to the
number of potentially significant composition variables. The “conservative halide rule” is more
effective in reducing the risk of salt formation but this comes at the significant expense of
excluding a wide range of formulations that do not form salt, which will result in decreased
waste loadings.

 52

 SECTION 7.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 Forty-six tests in two phases of testing were conducted on the DM10 melter system to
determine the concentrations of chloride that results in the formation of a secondary sulfate phase
on the glass pool surface for each of the six selected glass compositions at different sulfur
contents. The testing demonstrated the impact of chlorine on sulfate phase formation in the
alkali-limited region, the region in which waste loading is limited by sulfate, and for the highest
anticipated concentrations of both chromium and phosphate.

 Higher waste loading glass formulations that were developed for the LAW AP-101
(ORPLG27), AN-105 (ORPLA38-1 and ORPLA20), AN-102 (ORPLD6), AN-107 (ORPBL4),
and AZ-101 (ORPLE12) tank compositions were evaluated. For each waste composition and
sulfur content, the chloride concentration was progressively increased to the point at which a
sulfate salt phase develops, indicating the limit of sulfate incorporation for that particular
formulation. The tests were conducted at nominal WTP LAW processing conditions: 1150C
glass temperature, glass production rate of 2000 - 2250 kg/m2/day, complete cold cap, and sugar
additions to the feed corresponding to a stoichiometric ratio of 0.5 (1 mole sucrose per 16 moles
NOx). The halide tolerance for each waste composition based on these tests is summarized in
Tables 6.1 and 6.2. Data from the present and previous [7-9] tests on these six formulations with
the nominal levels of phosphate and chromate are plotted in Figures 6.1 and 6.2, which show that
the data fall along two trends, one for the low-potassium formulations (~0.5 wt% K2O) and the
other for the high-potassium formulation (~5.7 wt% K2O). There is a clear and substantial offset
between these trends, illustrating the significant effect of potassium. While these trends serve to
illustrate the relative effects of sulfate and chloride on salt formation in these high waste loading
glass formulations, they will shift with variations in phosphate and chromate, as well as with
other glass constituents. The new data from the two glasses tested in the present work all relate to
the low-potassium region and are reasonably consistent with the trends developed from the four
glasses tested in Phase 1. Significant exceptions are the data for tests 6B and 6F, which would be
predicted to be salt-free but did show a salt phase. A review of the data showed no anomalies in
these tests and although the salt phase was detected on only two out of three dip samples for both
tests, the salt was clearly present. These tests are two of the eight tests with the highest fluorine
concentrations (target of 0.47 wt% versus 0 - 0.2 wt% for the rest of the tests), which may
account for part of the discrepancy; however, previous work has suggested that the effect of
fluorine is significantly smaller than that of chlorine [14, 15]. Additional testing would be useful
to verify these data points and better define the effect of fluorine on salt formation.

 One of the objectives of the present tests was to expand the data set to provide an
improved basis for the development of models for predicting salt formation. The combined data
generated from the BNI baseline glass compositions [14, 15], the high waste loading LAW glass
compositions [5-9], and from the present two phases of testing [16, 17] consist of 147 tests
conducted on the DM10 and DM100 with seven waste compositions and twenty-one different

 53

glass compositions. The data span a wide compositional range and, as a result, analysis of the
full data set will require consideration of the potentially significant effects of other glass
composition variables besides sulfate, halides, phosphate, and chromate. Nevertheless, testing on
both WTP baseline and the newer high waste loading glass formulations has clearly shown that
increasing the concentrations of sulfur, chloride, chromium, and to a lesser degree fluorine,
increases the likelihood of secondary phase formation for each glass formulation.

 The combined melter test dataset was used to evaluate the performance of several
proposed rules for predicting salt formation based on composition [13, 18, 19]. None of the
proposed rules are effective in clearly separating compositions that form salt from those that do
not and they would therefore not be appropriate for controlling WTP operations. This is due in
part to the still rather limited data set in comparison to the number of potentially significant
composition variables. The “conservative halide rule” [19] is more effective in reducing the risk
of salt formation but this comes at the significant expense of excluding a wide range of
formulations that do not form salt, which will result in decreased waste loadings.

 Crucible scale glass formulation studies were conducted to screen and identify potential
additives that could be beneficial in improving sulfur and chlorine loadings in LAW glasses. The
tests were conducted in a sealed crucible to avoid loss of volatile components. Various additives
at a concentration of about 5 wt% were initially tested in the ORPLA20 glass composition. The
additives that were used in these screening tests were NiO, CoO MnO/MnO2, SnO2, Fe3O4,
Fe2O3, CuO/Cu2O, V2O5, and P2O5. The 5 wt% of additive was accommodated by removing
MgO, SnO2, Fe2O3 and part of the CaO from the ORPLA20 glass composition. In addition,
sufficient amounts of NaCl and Na2SO4 were added to the batch to ensure excesses of Cl and S
such that a molten salt phase would be present on the surface of the molten glass. The Na2O and
SiO2 concentrations in the glass were maintained at the target levels by adjusting the Na2SiO3
and SiO2 additions to the batch. Results from the screening tests showed that most of the glass
samples contained about 0.35 to 0.4 wt% SO3 and from 0.63 to 0.66 wt% Cl. Glass from the test
with Cu2O contained slightly higher SO3 and Cl contents of 0.44 wt% and 0.68 wt%,
respectively. In contrast, glass from the test with V2O5 contained 0.6 wt% SO3 and nearly
0.8 wt% Cl, both of which are substantially above the values observed with the other additives.
Consequently, V2O5 and Cu2O were selected for further investigation of sulfate solubility at
various chloride concentrations. In addition, considering the significant presence of P2O5 in
Hanford LAW and the beneficial effect of P2O5 in the mitigation of sulfate salt formation
observed in melter tests [14, 15, 25, 26], P2O5 was also included in subsequent tests.

 The results of sulfur solubility tests at various chloride concentrations showed decreases
in sulfate solubility with increases in chloride levels for all three additives (V2O5, P2O5 and
Cu2O) and the ORPLA20 base glass. V2O5 was clearly the most effective additive in mitigating
sulfate salt formation in the ORPLA20 glass composition. At zero, mid, and high concentrations
of chloride (Cl from 0 to 0.4 to 0.7 wt%), glass melts with 5 wt% V2O5 dissolved from 0.6 wt%
(high-Cl glass) to more than 0.8 wt% SO3 (Cl-free glass) as compared to roughly 0.4 wt% (high-
Cl glass) to 0.6 wt% SO3 (Cl-free glass) for the other additives. More importantly, the sulfate
solubility when compared at equal sulfate chemical potential is approximately 75% higher in
melts with V2O5 as compared to melts without V2O5. The maximum amount of SO3 that can be

 54

accommodated by ORPLA20 with 5 wt% V2O5 is estimated to be about 1.2 wt% as compared to
about 0.7 wt% with other additives.

 Analysis of the equilibrium salt phases showed that both V2O5 and P2O5 are present in
significant amounts in the salt phase, which, by dilution, tends to decrease the sulfate activity and
therefore also its concentration in the equilibrium glass melt. However, V2O5 also decreases the
activity coefficient of sulfate in the glass melt (as indicated by the significantly higher Henry
constant (Figure 3.6)), which tends to increase its concentration, and this latter effect
overwhelms the former. In contrast to its very large effect on sulfate solubility, the effect of V2O5
on chloride solubility was similar to that for P2O5, Cu2O, or simply the baseline glass frit, all of
which showed slightly higher Cl concentrations than the rest of the additives tested in the
screening experiments. Similar to sulfate solubility, the solubility of Cl also appears to obey
Henry’s law but with no apparent distinction between the Henry constant with the various
additives.

 Based on the sealed crucible test results, a modified ORPLA20 glass with 5 wt% V2O5
was prepared and characterized for properties relevant to processing and product quality (PCT,
VHT, viscosity, electrical conductivity, crystallization). The modified glass met all of the
processing and product quality requirements, although the PCT releases and VHT alteration were
significantly higher than those for the ORPLA20 base glass and close to the WTP limits. The
leaching performance of this glass (ORPLA20HV) which was not optimized in this work, can
potentially be improved through composition refinement in the future. VGF tests of the modified
ORPLA20 glass formulation with V2O5 indicated good feed processing characteristics. The
results of the sealed crucible studies showed that vanadium is by far the most effective of all of
the tested additives for reducing the tendency for sulfate-chloride salt formation. These results
are consistent with earlier findings with respect to sulfate salt formation [25, 26, 66-70], which
led to the use of V2O5 in many of the high waste loading LAW glass formulations that were
developed at VSL for ORP. The results also show potential to refine LAW glass formulations to
accommodate higher concentrations of sulfur and chlorine while mitigating salt formation.

 55
